                                                                                  High-rate qLDPC processors

                                                 Aditya Bhardwaj* ,1, † Muzhou Ma* ,1, ‡ Nadine Meister,1 Robbie King,2 Dolev Bluvstein,2
                                                        John Preskill,1, 2 Madelyn Cain,2 Qian Xu,2, S and Hsin-Yuan Huang2, 1, ¶
                                                                   1
                                                                       California Institute of Technology, Pasadena, CA 91125, USA
                                                                                  2
                                                                                    Oratomic, Pasadena, CA 91125, USA
                                                                                           (Dated: August 3, 2026)
                                                        Despite significant progress on quantum low-density parity-check (qLDPC) codes, build-
                                                     ing qLDPC processors that are high-rate, high-throughput, hardware-friendly, and fast-to-
                                                     decode remains a challenge. In this work, we introduce mitten codes, a family of qLDPC
                                                     processor codes with encoding rate 20% and check weight 9, constructed from non-abelian
                                                     groups. The non-abelian structure evades stringent distance bounds suffered by their abelian
                                                     counterparts, allowing mitten codes to reach distance 18 and beyond with only a few hun-
                                                     dred data qubits. The logical operators of a mitten code are related by the underlying group
                                                     action, and this symmetry yields a modular, low-overhead logical toolkit: full Clifford op-
                                                     erations follow from bridging just two reusable seed surgery gadgets of tens of qubits each,
                                                     or from a single fixed extractor. Furthermore, qLDPC processors based on mitten codes
                                                     support high-rate surgery that executes many logical measurements in parallel, and parallel
                                                     magic-state injection into all logical qubits at once. Under circuit-level depolarizing noise, our
                                                     fast decoder shows, without extrapolation, that the J300, 60, 14K code attains a block logical
arXiv:2607.28795v1 [quant-ph] 30 Jul 2026




                                                     error rate of ∼10−11 per round at 0.1% physical error rate (PER), while the J975, 195, ≤ 24K
                                                     code reaches ∼10−8 at 0.4% PER. Directly decoding 15 billion surgery experiments on the
                                                     J540, 108, 18K code at 0.1% PER, we observe only two logical failures, thereby demonstrating
                                                     a qLDPC processor capable of running ∼1010 logical operations. Our decoder achieves this
                                                     accuracy while being compatible with sub-millisecond average latency per logical cycle, suf-
                                                     ficient for real-time decoding on neutral atom hardware. Discovered by an end-to-end design
                                                     pipeline built on sQetch, a distance estimator orders of magnitude faster than existing tools,
                                                     and mapping efficiently onto near-term neutral atom and superconducting hardware, mitten
                                                     codes open a practical path toward fault-tolerant quantum computation.




                                                                                           I.   Introduction

                                               Useful quantum algorithms call for operations with logical error rates orders of magnitude below
                                            the physical error rates that can be achieved natively in hardware [1–14], making fault tolerance
                                            essential for practical quantum computing [15, 16]. The surface code [17–22] has been widely studied
                                            as a potential route to fault-tolerance because it can tolerate relatively high physical error rates [23, 24]
                                            and requires only geometrically local processing in a two-dimensional layout [25–27]. However, despite
                                            significant theoretical progress on variants of the surface code [28, 29], its low encoding rate entails
                                            a daunting overhead cost [29–31]. If instead geometrically nonlocal operations are permitted during
                                            error syndrome extraction [32–37], then high-rate quantum low-density parity-check (qLDPC) codes
                                            can substantially reduce this overhead cost in a quantum memory [38–53].
                                               But a quantum processor is more than just a memory; its utility is determined by the number of
                                            logical operations it can execute per unit time and per physical qubit without logical error. Factors
                                            contributing to this include the footprint of the code and its logical gadgets, the parallelism of logical
                                            operations, the clock speed of syndrome extraction, and the achievable error suppression. An archi-
                                            tecture that optimizes one factor in isolation might still be impractical if the others lag far behind.
                                            Thus, the designer of a fault-tolerant quantum processor seeks to satisfy the following four desiderata

simultaneously:
        1. High encoding rate: The number of physical qubits per logical qubit should be kept low.

        2. High throughput: The processor should support flexible logical operations acting on pro-
           grammable sets of logical qubits, so that many operations can be performed per unit time.

        3. Hardware compatibility: The processor should map efficiently onto physical platforms.

        4. Fast and accurate decoding: The processor should be decodable quickly and accurately, during
           both storage and logical operations.
   In this work, we introduce mitten codes 1 , a family of non-abelian lifted product codes2 [41, 42, 54]
that furnish quantum processors satisfying all four desiderata (Fig. 1). Mitten codes are built from

  The name “mitten codes” derives from the block structure of the check matrices. The 𝑋 and 𝑍 checks involve the

2
  Lifted product codes constructed from classical base matrices over F2 [𝐺] are a special case of balanced product
  codes [41] that can be constructed from more generic chain complexes whose chain groups need not be free modules.

1 × 2 base matrices over the group algebra F2 [𝐺] of a non-abelian group 𝐺. Comparing the number
of physical qubits to the number of checks guarantees an encoding rate of at least 20%, and allowing
𝐺 to be non-abelian yields codes that surpass the distance-6 upper bound suffered by all abelian
constructions with the same base-matrix shape (Section III). Remarkably, they do so at small block
sizes, achieving distances ≳ 20 with just hundreds of data qubits. Their modest size not only facilitates
experimental implementation and fast decoding, but also enables low-overhead and highly parallel
logical operations that could otherwise be challenging to realize on larger codes [51, 55–58]. A large
processor can instead be assembled by tiling many such identical blocks, which can have advantages
for fault-tolerant operation compared to a single monolithic quantum device.
    A central structural feature of the mitten codes is a highly symmetric canonical logical basis
which enables low-overhead and parallelizable fault-tolerant quantum processing. Whenever the base
matrices satisfy a square invertibility condition (Definition 16), one can explicitly construct low-
weight conjugate pairs of logical operators {(𝑋     ¯ 𝑔 , 𝑍¯𝑔 )}𝑔∈𝐺 representing the 𝑘 = |𝐺| encoded logical
qubits, such that every logical 𝑋  ¯ 𝑔 (𝑍¯𝑔 ) is carried to any other logical 𝑋   ¯ ℎ (𝑍¯ℎ ) through the group
action of 𝐺. Because all logical representatives are related by group symmetry, we can build a
modular, low-overhead surgery toolkit: just five reusable gadgets, themselves built from only two
seed gadgets, suffice for universal Clifford processing. Alternatively, we may trade this modularity
for expressiveness with a fixed full extractor [59] that can directly measure any logical Pauli product.
The same symmetry enables high-throughput logic through parallel surgery [55, 60] and parallel magic
state injection, the latter of which provides the remaining non-Clifford resource needed for universal
quantum computation. With our telescoping decoder, each window of 𝑂(𝑑) syndrome extraction

Figure 3: High-rate qLDPC processor discovery pipeline. Starting from the processor targets of
Definition 3, the pipeline narrows the design space in three levels. The first level explores the space of groups
and base matrices: theoretical distance bounds (Appendix G) first constrain the search space, and the fast code-
distance estimator sQetch (Appendix H) then brute-force searches the remaining space to find codes with good
parameters and addressable logical operations. The second level searches for fault-tolerant syndrome extraction
schedules and verifies logical performance through circuit-level simulations decoded with our telescoping decoder
(Appendix I). The third level constructs gadgets for instruction sets (ℐ𝐵 , ℐ𝐻 , ℐ𝐹 ), and optimizes for hardware
implementation on atom array and superconducting qubit platforms to obtain the final processor design. Dashed
arrows indicate feedback: candidates that fail downstream validation trigger retuning of the group or base
matrices upstream. The toolkit for our pipeline is open-sourced.


rounds can be decoded with sub-millisecond average latency in both memory and surgery experiments
(Section V, Table X), where 𝑑 is the code distance, while attaining very low logical error rates (Fig. 2).
Finally, the structure of the code aligns naturally with parallel block moves of atoms in neutral-
atom arrays, and for superconducting platforms we show that mitten codes have a similar hardware
complexity [36] to the bivariate bicycle codes [50] of comparable block size while achieving much
higher encoding rate.
    Mitten codes, however, form a vast family, and building a practical device requires singling out
explicit instances and equipping them with concrete gadgets, syndrome extraction schedules, and hard-
ware layouts. Our second main contribution is a qLDPC processor discovery pipeline (Section VII)
that builds on our theoretical results and turns a target specification of code parameters, logical error
rate, gadget footprint, and hardware constraints into a complete qLDPC processor design (Fig. 3).
Running the pipeline end to end produced the mitten codes with 20% encoding rate and check weight
9 listed in Table I. This was made tractable by sQetch [61], our new GPU-based distance estimator,
which is up to 800,000 times faster than current state-of-the-art methods (Appendix H).
    The remainder of the text is organized as follows. We first formalize what we require of a qLDPC
processor and the parameters by which we evaluate one (Section II). We then present mitten codes
together with their canonical logical basis (Section III), and show how this structure enables low-
overhead gadgets that turn them into qLDPC processors (Section IV). Next, we establish their per-
formance under full circuit-level decoding experiments with our new telescoping decoder (Section V)
and demonstrate how they may be implemented on both neutral atom and superconducting qubit
platforms (Section VI). Finally, we present our qLDPC processor discovery pipeline that produced
the codes (Section VII) and the accoutrements needed to convert them into qLDPC processors.
                                                                                                                       5


                              II.   Metrics for fault-tolerant qLDPC processors

   Fault tolerance is achieved by encoding logical information in quantum error-correcting codes, but
codes alone only protect information. To process information, a code must be equipped with an
instruction set, together with the supporting gadgets and a decoder. In this section, we formalize this
notion of a fault-tolerant quantum processor and present three parameters by which we characterize
the performance of a quantum processor.

Definition 1 (Quantum instruction set (informal version of Definition 17)). A quantum instruction
set ℐ of a fault-tolerant quantum processor is a set of instructions, each a logical operation together
with its physical gadget realization, that supports universal fault-tolerant quantum computation.

Quantum instruction sets are not unique. We consider three, each designed for a different objective.
The basic instruction set ℐ𝐵 (Definition 18) consists of fault-tolerant gadgets for single-qubit logical
Pauli measurements, weight-two Pauli product measurements (PPMs), and noisy 𝑇 -state injection;
these compile any Clifford+𝑇 circuit [22, 62–66], and the noisy 𝑇 -states are distilled by inter-block
transversal3 [64, 67] or intra-block [68] distillation. Since distillation uses only the fault-tolerant Pauli
measurements, the magic state distillation process is also fault-tolerant.
    The high-throughput instruction set ℐ𝐻 (Definition 19) generalizes the basic instruction set to also
include high-rate gadgets for parallel Pauli product measurements and parallel 𝑇 -state injection. This
suits architectures where the time overhead is as important as the space overhead [56, 69, 70]. The
fixed-gadget instruction set ℐ𝐹 (Definition 20) consists of a single fixed extractor [59, 71] that measures
arbitrary Pauli products selected in software, plus noisy 𝑇 -state injection. Because the connectivity
of the extractor is fixed, it suits platforms with rigid connectivity.
    The three instruction sets serve different purposes and can be combined; as we show in Section IV,
the mitten codes support all three at low overhead. In particular, the entire basic instruction set
can be realized with only 5 reusable gadgets. The formal definitions of each instruction set are in
Appendix C. A processor can be characterized in terms of its instruction set and hardware capabilities.

Definition 2 (quop). Given a quantum instruction set ℐ, call an instruction primitive if it is not
a parallel composition of other instructions in ℐ. A quantum operation ( quop) is the execution of a
single primitive instruction; an instruction composed of 𝑚 primitive instructions executed in parallel
counts as 𝑚 quops.

Under this definition, every instruction in the basic instruction set ℐ𝐵 counts as a single quop, while
instructions in the high-throughput instruction set ℐ𝐻 that perform 𝑚 weight-1 or weight-two PPMs
or inject 𝑚 magic states in parallel count as 𝑚 quops. In the fixed-gadget instruction set ℐ𝐹 an
arbitrary PPM performed by the extractor counts as a single quop.4 With the notion of a quop in
hand, we now define three parameters that characterize a universal fault-tolerant qLDPC processor.

Definition 3 (Parameters of a universal fault-tolerant qLDPC processor). Fix a qLDPC processor
with a quantum instruction set ℐ and a decoder. We define:

     1. Processing capacity: The expected number of quops that the processor can execute without any
        logical error; for a logical error rate 𝑝L per quop, the processing capacity is 1/𝑝L .

     2. Throughput: The maximum number of quops that the processor can execute within a single
        logical cycle consisting of 𝑅 rounds of syndrome extraction, where the most standard protocols
        require 𝑅 = 𝑂(𝑑).

3
    Since all CSS codes support transversal CNOT gates, any instruction set based on a CSS code naturally includes it.
4
    While the ability to perform arbitrary Pauli-product measurements can lead to more efficient compilations of algo-
    rithms, the aim of our definitions is to characterize a processor in a manner agnostic of any algorithm. We therefore
    count every arbitrary Pauli-product measurement in ℐ𝐹 as one quop regardless of its weight; the compilation savings
    appear instead in the number of quops an algorithm requires under different instruction sets.
                                                                                                                     6


   3. Cycle time: The time to complete one syndrome extraction (SE) cycle.

The three parameters are set by distinct design choices. Processing capacity demands resilience to
noise during both storage and processing. This favors a low physical error rate, a small code block
length, and low gadget overhead: fewer and less faulty components mean fewer errors to correct, and
a smaller decoding problem that the decoder can solve quickly and accurately. Throughput is driven
by the encoding rate and the parallelism of the logical layer. The cycle time is set by the time to
complete one round of syndrome extraction, which can vary significantly not only across hardware
platforms but also across syndrome extraction schedules; the latter is especially relevant when the
schedules must be realized by non-local qubit movements such as on neutral atom arrays. Throughput
and cycle time jointly set the logical operation rate, i.e. the number of quops executed per unit time,
and can sometimes partially compensate for one another. Processing capacity, by contrast, caps the
total number of operations executed reliably, and no gain in operation rate can remedy a processor
with low processing capacity.


                                                    III.     Mitten codes

   We present mitten codes, a family of qLDPC codes that furnish universal fault-tolerant qLDPC
processors performing well on all three parameters in Definition 3.

Definition 4 (Mitten codes). Mitten codes are a family of lifted product codes LP(𝐴, 𝐵) with check
weight 9 and encoding rate 20% whose classical base matrices 𝐴, 𝐵 ∈ F2 [𝐺]1×2 have the canonical
form

representations of ℛ = 𝐹2 [𝐺] (Definition 8), and we have labeled the blocks of data qubits, 𝑋-checks

right-regular representation 𝑅(𝑏1 ) to be full-rank matrices.

   The 1×2 shape of the classical base matrices guarantees a 20% encoding rate while the non-abelian
group is essential for achieving high distance. Note that if 𝐺 were abelian, the minimum-weight
codeword (𝑎1 , 𝑎0 )𝑇 of 𝐴 produces a 𝑍¯ logical operator (𝑎1 , 0, 𝑎0 , 0, 0)𝑇 , thus capping the distance of
LP(𝐴, 𝐵) at the check weight of its classical base matrices. Taking 𝐺 non-abelian removes this cap
and is what allows a small block to reach high distance. Using the discovery pipeline described in
Section VII, we obtain eight instances of mitten codes with parameters ranging from J150, 30, 10K
through J540, 108, 18K to J975, 195, ≤ 24K (Table I).
   Mitten codes compare favorably in encoding rate and block size with other leading qLDPC code
families. With a block size under a thousand, the J975, 195, ≤ 24K instance encodes 195 logical
qubits, whereas a rotated surface code of distance 24 would require over 100,000 physical qubits to
encode the same number. The bivariate bicycle codes [50] such as the J144, 12, 12K gross code and the

(4-AOD) of crossed AODs (further details in Table XI, Appendix J). 𝐶hw is the hardware complexity of the
code’s multilayer superconducting layout from HAL [36], normalized so a surface-code layout yields 𝐶hw = 1
regardless of its size; the full breakdown (routing tiers, coupler length, bump-bond transitions, through-silicon
vias) is in Table XII (Appendix J). For comparison, the J144, 12, 12K gross code achieves 𝐶hw = 2.12 and the
J288, 12, 18K two-gross code achieves 𝐶hw = 2.24.


J288, 12, 18K two-gross code, achieve encoding rates of only about 8% and 4%, respectively, compared
to the guaranteed 20% of mitten codes. Mitten codes also have smaller block sizes than high-rate
abelian LP codes [56] and Kasai codes [51, 57], simplifying decoding and facilitating low-overhead and
highly parallelizable logical operations.
   The low overhead and high parallelism of logical operations on mitten codes stem from the full-rank
requirement in Definition 4 which is a special case of the square invertibility condition of Definition 16.
This condition equips mitten codes with a canonical logical basis whose logical operators are related
by group action.

                                    IV.    Universal fault-tolerant logic

    We equip mitten codes for universal fault-tolerant quantum processing by utilizing the properties
of the canonical basis to construct gadgets that realize the three instruction sets ℐ𝐵 , ℐ𝐻 , and ℐ𝐹 .
    Reusable graph surgery for ℐ𝐵 . A graph surgery gadget [66, 73–77] measures a target logical
Pauli operator by temporarily attaching an ancilla system to the code block (Fig. 1(b)). The ancilla
system consists of ancilla qubits and new 𝑋 and 𝑍 stabilizer checks that couple the ancilla checks
to the support of the target logical. The new stabilizers are chosen so that a product of a subset
of them equals the target logical operator. Measuring these stabilizers reads out the target logical
measurement outcome while leaving the remaining encoded information intact. Because the canonical
logical basis lies in a single group orbit, one seed gadget for 𝑋       ¯ 𝑒 and one for 𝑍¯𝑒 , rewired by the group
                             ¯          ¯
action, can measure any 𝑋𝑔 and 𝑍𝑔 [78] for 𝑔 ∈ 𝐺. Furthermore, bridging seed gadgets [75] yields
equally reusable gadgets for 𝑋     ¯ ℎ , 𝑍¯𝑔 𝑍¯ℎ , and 𝑌¯𝑔 for any 𝑔, ℎ ∈ 𝐺. A mitten code therefore needs only
                                ¯𝑔 𝑋
five reusable gadgets for universal Clifford processing, and we construct all five explicitly and report
their overheads in Table II. All of the gadgets are distance-preserving and increase the maximum check
weight by at most three. Further details regarding gadget construction can be found in Appendix D.1
    Parallel surgery gadgets and parallel magic state injection for ℐ𝐻 . High-rate surgery gadgets
can measure up to 𝑘/2 disjoint weight-two logical Pauli products [55], covering all logical qubits at
once. We explicitly construct such gadgets measuring up to 𝑘/2 disjoint random Pauli products of
the form 𝑋     ¯ ℎ on the J300, 60, 14K mitten code. The low-weight canonical basis keeps the gadget
            ¯𝑔 𝑋
overheads modest, as reported in Table III. Detailed explanations of their construction can be found
in Appendix D.2. Next, we present a scheme for injecting 𝑘 magic states in parallel into a mitten
code. The magic states from the surface codes of distance 𝑑rep can be teleported to all logical qubits
of the mitten code in parallel utilizing an ancillary LP code and parallel surgeries that preserve the
LP structures. Notably, the extra space cost is dominated by the surface-code magic factory, and even
this cost is typically subleading over the course of a computation, since the factory’s ancilla qubits
can be substantially reused (Appendix E.3, Table V). The time overhead is Θ(𝑑rep ), independent of 𝑘.
In Appendix E we provide detailed analysis of the parallel magic injection protocol on mitten codes
and prove its end-to-end distance-preserving property.
    Full extractors for ℐ𝐹 . A full extractor [59] is a single fixed gadget that measures any logical Pauli
product by activating or deactivating individual connections to the code block. Full extractors are in
general costly in space, but the single-orbit canonical basis lets mitten codes reduce this cost. Using
techniques similar to those in Refs. [71, 79], we construct single-sided 𝑋 and 𝑍 extractors and join
them with a bridge into a full extractor. Across the mitten code family, attaching the extractor keeps
every merged check weight at most 14; full overheads are reported in Table IV and further details of
how we constructed the extractors can be found in Appendix D.


                V.    Decoding performance and processing capacity of mitten codes

   We now turn to the processing capacity of our mitten code qLDPC processors: the expected number
of quops they execute before the first logical error (Definition 3). Probing a processing capacity of
1010 quops requires simulating and decoding tens of billions of shots, each an independent noise
realization sampled from a circuit-level noise model with Stim [80]. Our decoding infrastructure
                                                                                                                       9


is therefore designed to meet two requirements at once: it must have enough throughput to probe
this low logical error rate regime, and its estimates should reflect a realistic decoding stack that can
support real-time decoding.5
    To satisfy both, we develop a telescoping decoder that applies many stages of belief propagation
(BP) and Relay-BP [83], followed by a final integer-programming stage that solves the most-likely-
error decoding problem exactly (Appendix I). The design builds on hierarchical decoding strategies
that route harder shots to progressively more accurate decoders [51, 84–86].6 Custom CUDA kernels
decode the vast majority of shots at high throughput on the GPU, and custom C kernels on the
CPU handle the harder shots the GPU stages defer [61]. In most of our simulations, later stages also
switch to a finer-grained but equivalent representation of the decoding problem given by the GARI
transform [87] of the full correlated detector error model. The GARI transform rewires the decoding
graph into a form more amenable to BP.
    Benchmarked on memory experiments of the J144, 12, 12K gross code [50], our telescoping decoder
matches or improves upon the logical error rates of current state-of-the-art decoders, including most-
likely-error approximators like Tesseract [88] and neural decoders like Cascade [89], while delivering
nearly twice the throughput of the neural decoder closest to it in accuracy (Figure 9, Table IX,
Appendix I).
    Figure 2 summarizes the performance of the mitten codes under our telescoping decoder. In all
simulations, we employ a circuit-level noise model with uniform depolarizing noise of strength 𝑝 applied
to state preparation, two-qubit gates, and measurements, and no idling noise. As a memory, the mitten
codes behave as a family with numerics consistent with a threshold7 of 𝑝𝑐 ≈ 0.7%. At 𝑝 = 0.1%, the
                                                            −7.9 × 10
J300, 60, 14K code reaches a block logical error rate of 9.5+21.8     −12 per syndrome extraction round,

corresponding to only one logical error observed in over 100 billion syndrome extraction rounds.
Additionally, at 𝑝 = 0.4%, the J975, 195, ≤ 24K code attains a block logical error rate of 4.2+5.5  −2.7 ×
10−8 per round, nearly two orders of magnitude below the 2.44+0.53  −0.44 ×  10−6 of the J112320, 195, 24K

stack of rotated surface codes decoded with minimum-weight perfect matching [90]. For all memory
experiments in Figure 2(a) we use SE circuits that preserve the block-wise group structure, reflecting
the experimental realization on atom-array platforms. Within this block-wise family, we used our new
distance estimator sQetch [61] to search for schedules that likely preserve the circuit-level distance.
The surgery experiments in Figure 2(b) instead use random coloration SE circuits, for which we
verified with sQetch that the circuit-level distance is likewise preserved (Appendix H, Appendix J).
    Figure 2(b) shows the performance of fault-tolerant logical operations at 𝑝 = 0.1%. For the 𝑋-type
surgeries we simulate both the 𝑋 basis, which contains the measured logical operator, and the 𝑍 basis,
confirming that the unmeasured 𝑍 logicals are not disturbed by the surgery. On the J300, 60, 14K code
we simulate single-logical 𝑋 ¯ measurements, joint 𝑋  ¯𝑋 ¯ measurements, and high-rate surgery gadgets
                ¯  ¯
measuring ten 𝑋 𝑋 products in parallel, observing two logical errors across one billion 𝑋  ¯𝑋 ¯ operations
and none in the remaining experiments. This supports the conclusion that the J300, 60, 14K mitten
code can function as a giga-quop processor at 𝑝 = 0.1%. The J540, 108, 18K code incurs only two logical
errors across 15 billion 𝑋¯𝑋¯ operations corresponding to a logical error rate of 1.33+1.76 × 10−10 per
                                                                                        −0.86
quop. This is consistent with the conclusion that it can function as a ten-billion-quop processor.
Notably, every logical error we observe in the 𝑋 basis is a timelike error in the measured operator itself
so these error rates can likely be suppressed further by simply increasing the number of syndrome
extraction rounds. Finally, although the pipeline is tuned to maximize Monte Carlo throughput,

5
  By real-time decoding we mean meeting the reaction-time requirement, not merely avoiding the backlog problem [81].
  The backlog problem can always be avoided by adding compute to each stage of the pipeline via parallel window
  decoding [82]. However, a feedforward operation conditioned on a decoded logical measurement must wait for its
  window to be decoded, so what matters is the reaction time: the latency to decode a single window (Appendix I).
6
  We call our design telescoping rather than the more common hierarchical to emphasize its many nested stages of BP
  and Relay-BP: each stage winnows away the shots it can confidently decode, so that progressively more expensive
  decoders act on a progressively smaller residual of harder shots. This narrowing allows us to “telescope” in to the low
  logical error rate regime.
7
  At these finite block sizes, the common crossing of the logical error rate curves should be understood as an effective,
  finite-size threshold rather than an asymptotic one.

                                                                 VI.         Hardware implementations

    To realize a mitten code processor, the nonlocal connectivity of the code must also be implemented
on physical hardware. We focus on neutral atom and superconducting qubit platforms, which face
different challenges. On neutral atom arrays, nonlocal connectivity is naturally realized by coherent
atom transport [34, 35, 91–94], and the challenge is temporal: movement schedules must be optimized
so that transport does not inflate the cycle time of the processor. On superconducting chips, the
cycle time is already low (microsecond scale) [26], and the challenge is instead spatial: the nonlocal
connectivity must be laid out as fixed couplers routed on a multilayer chip [37, 95–97]. We address
both challenges and demonstrate how mitten codes can be realized on both hardware platforms with
competitive cycle times on neutral atoms and hardware complexities comparable to bivariate bicycle
codes on superconducting hardware.


                                                                                 A.   Neutral atoms

   Our neutral atom SE protocol establishes the nonlocal connectivity by moving the ancilla atoms
while keeping the data atoms stationary. For each entangling gate, the ancilla atom must be brought
adjacent to the corresponding data atom. The exact movement is determined by the check matrices
𝐻𝑋 and 𝐻𝑍 in Eq. (2), whose ring entries (𝑎0 , 𝑎1 , 𝑏0 , 𝑏1 ) are sums of group elements 𝑔 ∈ 𝐺. Each
group element 𝑔 defines a gate layer between one check block (𝑋0 , 𝑋1 , 𝑍0 , or 𝑍1 ) and one data block
(𝐷1 . . . , 𝐷5 ).
   Once the ordering is chosen, moving between consecutive gate layers requires permuting the ancilla
atoms within each check block according to multiplication by a group element. On current neutral
atom platforms, atom transport is typically implemented using a crossed pair of acousto-optic deflec-
tors (AODs), which move entire rows and columns of atoms in parallel [35, 91, 98, 99]. The group
structure of the mitten codes makes the required permutations highly structured and compatible with
                                                                                                         11


these AOD restrictions. When the group 𝐺 can be decomposed as a direct product 𝐺 = 𝐺1 × 𝐺2 , the
atoms can be arranged on a 2D grid with 𝐺1 on one axis and 𝐺2 on the other. Then, multiplication by
a group element decomposes into row and column permutations of the |𝐺|-atom check block. Thus,
these permutations directly match the movements allowed under crossed AODs. Semidirect products
admit a similar but twisted movement structure, described in more detail in Appendix J.1.a.
   To explicitly specify the movement rules, we first label the |𝐺| atoms in each check and data
block by the elements of 𝐺. Recall that the first ring element is 𝑎0 = 𝑔1 + 𝑔2 + 𝑔3 . In 𝐻𝑋 , 𝐿(𝑎0 ) =
𝐿(𝑔1 )+𝐿(𝑔2 )+𝐿(𝑔3 ) couples 𝑋0 and 𝑋1 to 𝐷1 and 𝐷2 , respectively, with each term in the sum defining
a separate gate layer. Consider two consecutive layers 𝐿(𝑔𝑖 ) and 𝐿(𝑔𝑗 ). During layer 𝐿(𝑔𝑖 ), check atom
𝑘 ∈ 𝐺 is adjacent to data atom ℎ = 𝑔𝑖−1 𝑘, since a 1 in the binary permutation matrix 𝐿(𝑔𝑖 )𝑘,ℎ = 1
corresponds to 𝑘 = 𝑔𝑖 ℎ. To then perform layer 𝐿(𝑔𝑗 ), the same check atom must move to ℎ′ = 𝑔𝑗−1 𝑘.
The required rearrangement of check blocks 𝑋0 and 𝑋1 is therefore the relative permutation 𝐿(𝑔𝑗−1 𝑔𝑖 ),
because left multiplication by 𝑔𝑗−1 𝑔𝑖 sends ℎ = 𝑔𝑖−1 𝑘 to ℎ′ = 𝑔𝑗−1 𝑘. Analogously, a transition from
𝑅(𝑔𝑖 ) to 𝑅(𝑔𝑗 ) requires the permutation 𝑅(𝑔𝑗−1 𝑔𝑖 ). Repeating this process for all group elements in
the check matrices completes one syndrome extraction cycle.
   We show an example move in Fig. 4(a) for the J150, 30, 10K code, whose SE cycle video is also
available at Ref. [61]. Here, the group is 𝐺 = 𝐶5 × 𝑆3 , so we arrange each |𝐺| = 30-atom block on a
5 × 6 grid, with rows indexed by 𝐶5 elements and columns by 𝑆3 elements. Thus, each of the |𝐺| = 30
atoms are labeled by a group element (𝑥𝑎 , 𝜎) ∈ 𝐶5 ×𝑆3 where 𝑥 is the generator of 𝐶5 , 𝑎 ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3, 4},
and 𝜎 ∈ 𝑆3 . We show one transition from the hook-free SE schedule, which takes check blocks 𝑋0
and 𝑋1 from layer 𝐿(𝑔𝑖 ) = 𝐿(𝑟2 ) to layer 𝐿(𝑔𝑗 ) = 𝐿(𝑥 · 𝑠𝑟2 ). Here, the reflection 𝑠 and the rotation
𝑟 are the generators of 𝑆3 . Thus, the relative permutation to perform is 𝐿(𝑔𝑗−1 𝑔𝑖 ) = 𝐿(𝑥−1 · 𝑠𝑟). This
factorizes into the 𝑆3 column permutation 𝐿(𝑠𝑟) (Fig. 4(a)ii) and the 𝐶5 row shift 𝐿(𝑥−1 ) (Fig. 4(a)iii).
𝐿(𝑠𝑟) simply swaps the 3 left and right columns, and 𝐿(𝑥−1 ) is just a cyclic shift of the rows by 1.
Each move can be implemented in a single transport step using two pairs of crossed AODs.
   For the mitten codes in Table I, we estimate syndrome extraction (SE) cycle times using present
day AOD capabilities and experimentally demonstrated transport data from Ref. [91]. This hardware
model follows that of [51]. For the codes listed in Table I, we find that a single SE cycle takes about
8 − 24 ms with 2 pairs of crossed AODs, and 5 − 15 ms with 4 pairs of crossed AODs.
   To isolate the overhead imposed by the AOD movement restrictions, we also consider a futuristic
architecture in which every atom can follow an independently controlled trajectory. Such control
could potentially be implemented using new and developing optical instruments other than AODs,
such as a fast spatial light modulator (SLM) [100, 101]. As a comparison, we use the same transport
parameters as in the AOD estimates, and find that every code admits an SE cycle time between
2.9 − 4.6 ms (see Table XI). As this is a forward looking device, the raw SE cycle times are not to be
emphasized, but rather its comparison with the AOD-constrained cycle times.
   Finally, we want to emphasize movement speed is a tunable parameter depending on the desired
performance. In the idealized regime where finite trap depth, rather than AOD technical limitations,
sets the maximum acceleration, a move which begins and ends at rest, takes time approximately
𝑡 ∝ 𝐼 −1/2 , where I is the intensity of the tweezer. (See Appendix J.1.d for more details.) As such, to
reduce movement times by a factor of 𝑛, one can, in theory, increase the power-per-trap by a factor
of 𝑛2 . However, if laser power limits the number of qubits, this reduces the number of atoms that
can be transported simultaneously by a factor of 𝑛2 . Conversely, parallel protocols, such as Shor-style
syndrome extraction [102], could also reduce SE times. So, while SE times can readily be reduced
with neutral atoms, both through using more laser power per-atom or through Shor-style SE, true
hardware implementations will require a more holistic analysis in terms of space and time.


                                      B.   Superconducting qubits

   Our smaller mitten codes are also well suited for superconducting qubit architectures, where the
code’s connectivity can be fabricated as couplers routed across the tiers of a multilayer chip. The
                                                                                                     12


simplest proxy for this fabrication cost is the planar thickness of the code’s Tanner graph i.e. the
minimum number of planar layers into which its edges can be decomposed. We prove that the thickness
of every mitten code is exactly three (Theorem 15). This is one layer more than the thickness-2
bivariate bicycle codes [50].
    Thickness, however, is only a coarse proxy for superconducting hardware complexity; a finer as-
sessment requires fixing a concrete hardware architecture. We choose a multi-chip stackup [36] with
flip-chip geometry [95–97, 103] in which the qubits occupy the first tier, and each additional chip cre-
ates a higher routing tier that provides more space to route nonlocal couplers without crossings. The
cost of a layout [36] is then set by the number of tiers it uses, the length of each routed coupler, and
the amount of vertical routing required through bump-bond transitions [96, 103] and through-silicon
vias [95, 104, 105].
    As a first step towards evaluating the hardware feasibility of the mitten codes beyond their thick-
ness, we lay out each code with HAL [36], a heuristic placement-and-routing algorithm for qLDPC
codes on multilayer superconducting hardware. Although HAL can choose the first-tier qubit place-
ment automatically, we find that supplying a custom layout is crucial. We place all the qubits on the
first tier as a grid of 3 × 3 modules from our thickness-3 decomposition (Figure 13), and optimize
the module placement as a quadratic assignment problem. HAL then routes the remaining couplers
through higher tiers while avoiding collisions (Appendix J.2).
    We summarize the resulting layouts by the hardware complexity 𝐶hw defined in Ref. [36], which
is a weighted average of the four metrics defined above. These metrics are linearly rescaled so that
a surface-code layout yields 𝐶hw = 1 and a layout saturating the optimistic hardware targets of
Ref. [36]—five tiers, coupler lengths of ten times the nearest-neighbor distance, four bump-bond
transitions and three TSVs per coupler—yields 𝐶hw = 2. Table I lists 𝐶hw for each mitten code, and
Table XII gives the full breakdown into the individual metrics. Despite their larger thickness, the
smaller mitten codes achieve hardware complexity similar to or lower than bivariate bicycle codes of
comparable block size while encoding substantially more logical qubits.


                            VII.   QLDPC processor discovery pipeline

   We have shown that mitten codes enable qLDPC processors with high processing capacity and
throughput. However, building a practical device requires singling out explicit instances and equipping
them with concrete gadgets, syndrome extraction schedules, and hardware layouts. In this section, we
describe our pipeline for qLDPC processor discovery. The pipeline relies on two ingredients, theoretical
bounds that constrain the search space and sQetch [61], a fast GPU-based distance estimator that
runs up to 800,000× faster than current state-of-the-art methods [106] and allows us to brute-force
search through millions of candidate codes per hour on a single GPU.
   As shown in Figure 3, the first stage of the pipeline constrains the search space with filters that
avoid constructing the quantum code altogether. This is enabled by the following new theorem.

Theorem 2 ([informal version of Theorem 8]). Let 𝐴 and 𝐵 be wide base matrices over the group
algebra F2 [𝐺] of any group 𝐺 such that the binary matrices 𝐿(𝐴) and 𝑅(𝐵) have full row rank. Then
the minimum distance of the two classical base codes 𝐴 and 𝐵 upper bounds the distance of the lifted
product code LP(𝐴, 𝐵).

We also found a counterexample, presented in Appendix G, that demonstrates that one cannot in
general get rid of the full row rank condition in Theorem 2. Since every base matrix we search over
satisfies the full row rank condition (Remark 11), Theorem 2 allows us to just check the distance
of the significantly smaller classical base matrices before combining them to make a quantum code.
These classical distances are in turn upper bounded by a collection of purely algebraic bounds—the
commutator subgroup bound and the element order bound among many others (Appendix G)—
which are evaluated directly from the group 𝐺 and the base-matrix entries and allow us to rule out
candidates that cannot have high distance. Finally, we use sQetch to brute force search through the
remaining search space to pair classical codes up and construct mitten codes with high distance. We
                                                                                                     13


equip surviving codes with hook-error-free syndrome extraction schedules by searching via sQetch for
schedules that likely preserve the circuit-level distance. Afterwards, the surviving codes are dressed
with their surgery, extractor, and magic state injection gadgets, compiled to concrete atom-array
movement schedules and multilayer superconducting layouts, and finally simulated under circuit-level
depolarizing noise with the high-throughput telescoping decoder. A more detailed discussion of the
design of our qLDPC processor discovery pipeline is provided in Appendix F.


                                   VIII.   Conclusion and outlook

   We have introduced mitten codes, a family of 1/5-rate, check-weight-9 lifted product codes over
non-abelian groups with instances from J150, 30, 10K through J540, 108, 18K to J975, 195, ≤ 24K, and
shown that they enable universal fault-tolerant qLDPC processors that perform well by all three
metrics in Definition 3: processing capacity, throughput, and cycle time. This high performance
derives from the structured canonical logical basis admitted by these codes, which yields a basic
instruction set based on five reusable graph-surgery gadgets, a high-throughput instruction set with
parallel surgery gadgets and parallel magic state injection, and a fixed-gadget instruction set with
a low-overhead extractor. Decoded by our telescoping decoder, mitten codes attain a logical error
rate of 10−11 per syndrome extraction cycle in memory experiments, and achieve a logical processing
capacity consistent with 1010 quops, for a physical gate error rate of 𝑝 = 0.1%.
   These codes were found by our qLDPC processor discovery pipeline, powered by the GPU-based
distance estimator sQetch. Because every design requirement enters the pipeline as an adjustable
input, we anticipate its use well beyond the scope of this work, for co-designing processors suited for
other hardware platforms, rate targets, and instruction sets. Indeed, this same pipeline also produced
some of the quasi-cyclic LP codes that are reported in Ref. [56], as well as other abelian LP codes
described in Appendix G.
   Our telescoping decoder matches or improves upon the logical error rates of current state-of-the-
art decoders. Even so, our analysis of logical error rates has been limited by the classical computing
resources available for our surgery simulations; moreover, in the case of the J540, 108, 18K code, the
observed logical failures were exclusively due to timelike errors, indicating that increasing the number
of syndrome measurement rounds could further suppress the logical error rates. Higher-distance
mitten codes may well reach teraquop-scale processing capacity, a regime we have not yet been able
to certify by Monte Carlo sampling.
   Mitten codes and the qLDPC processor discovery pipeline open many opportunities for future
exploration. Further improvements in processor design may reduce the spacetime cost of surgery
gadgets. Decoders that exploit code structure more effectively may run faster and perform even
better. Better on-chip routing in superconducting processors and enhanced movement strategies in
atom arrays may reduce the complexity of syndrome extraction and logical operations. Finally, the
small block size, modest check weight, and rich symmetry of mitten codes may enable experimental
demonstrations of high-rate qLDPC processors on near-term devices.

                                             A.        Preliminaries

   We review the lifted product (LP) code construction [41, 54]. We begin with hypergraph prod-
uct (HGP) codes [38] and then show that the lifted product is a natural generalization of the HGP
construction obtained by replacing scalar binary matrices with matrices whose entries lie in a group
ring. We then recast both constructions from the perspective of homological algebra, where hyper-
graph product and lifted product codes can be described as tensor product chain complexes and their
logical operators associated with the homology of the chain complex. Throughout, we are interested
in error-correcting codes on qubits and hence always work over F2 .


                                  1.    Hypergraph product codes

   A classical linear code over F2 can be defined by a parity-check matrix 𝐻 ∈ F𝑟×𝑐
                                                                                  2 , where 𝑐 is the
number of bits and 𝑟 is the number of parity checks. The codewords are precisely the vectors in ker 𝐻.
A CSS code can be specified by two parity-check matrices 𝐻𝑋 and 𝐻𝑍 that satisfy the commutation
condition 𝐻𝑋 𝐻𝑍𝑇 = 0. The hypergraph product code construction gives a systematic way to construct
such pairs of parity-check matrices from two classical codes.
Definition 5 (Hypergraph product code). Let 𝐴 ∈ F𝑟21 ×𝑐1 and 𝐵 ∈ F𝑟22 ×𝑐2 . The hypergraph product
(HGP) code [38] associated with (𝐴, 𝐵) is the CSS code on 𝑛 = 𝑐1 𝑐2 + 𝑟1 𝑟2 physical qubits with
parity-check matrices
                                       𝐻𝑋 = 𝐴 ⊗ 𝐼𝑐2 ⃒ 𝐼𝑟1 ⊗ 𝐵 𝑇 ,                                (A1)
                                                  [︀          ⃒             ]︀

                                       𝐻𝑍 = 𝐼𝑐1 ⊗ 𝐵 ⃒ 𝐴𝑇 ⊗ 𝐼𝑟2 .                                 (A2)
                                                  [︀⃒         ]︀

   This definition ensures that the CSS commutation condition holds:
                       𝐻𝑋 𝐻𝑍𝑇 = (𝐴 ⊗ 𝐼𝑐2 )(𝐼𝑐1 ⊗ 𝐵)𝑇 + (𝐼𝑟1 ⊗ 𝐵 𝑇 )(𝐴𝑇 ⊗ 𝐼𝑟2 )𝑇                  (A3)
                               =𝐴⊗𝐵 +𝐴⊗𝐵 𝑇                𝑇
                                                                                                 (A4)
                               = 0.                                                              (A5)
where the last equality follows since we are working over F2 .
Definition 6 (Kernel dimensions). For a binary matrix 𝑀 , define
                           ℎ(𝑀 ) = dim ker 𝑀,                 ℎ⊥ (𝑀 ) = dim ker 𝑀 𝑇 .            (A6)
   The hypergraph product code has
                                      𝑘 = ℎ(𝐴)ℎ(𝐵) + ℎ⊥ (𝐴)ℎ⊥ (𝐵)                                (A7)
logical qubits. If 𝐴 and 𝐵 are full-row-rank parity-check matrices for classical codes of dimensions
ℎ(𝐴) and ℎ(𝐵), then ℎ⊥ (𝐴) = ℎ⊥ (𝐵) = 0 and the formula reduces to 𝑘 = ℎ(𝐴)ℎ(𝐵). We can also
obtain a simple lower bound on 𝑘 in terms of the dimensions of 𝐴 and 𝐵 assuming they have more
columns than rows:
                                         𝑘 ≥ (𝑐𝑎 − 𝑟𝑎 )(𝑐𝑏 − 𝑟𝑏 ).                               (A8)
Example 1. The unrotated surface code is an example of a hypergraph product between two repetition
codes.
   Let Rep(𝑑) denote the length-𝑑 repetition code with parity-check matrix
                                             1 10 ··· 0
                                         ⎛                              ⎞

                                         ⎜0 1 1 . . . ... ⎟
                                         ⎜                ⎟
                                                               (𝑑−1)×𝑑
                              𝐻rep (𝑑) = ⎜ . . . .        ⎟ ∈ F2       .
                                         ⎜                ⎟
                                         ⎜. .   .  .
                                         ⎝.   . . . 0⎠    ⎟
                                             0 ··· 0              1 1
                                                                                                      23


Taking 𝐴 = 𝐵 = 𝐻rep (𝑑) in the hypergraph product construction gives the distance-𝑑 unrotated surface
code with parameters J𝑑2 + (𝑑 − 1)2 , 1, 𝑑K.

   LP codes generalize HGP codes by replacing binary matrix entries with elements of a group algebra.
Thus, before introducing lifted product codes, we first fix some group conventions we use to describe
our code families, and then review the group theory background needed for the construction.


                             2.   Group conventions and group algebras

    Many of the groups used in our code constructions can be written as direct products or semidirect
products. As the definition for a direct product is unambiguous, we just specify the convention used
for semidirect product.

Definition 7 (Semidirect product). Let 𝑁 and 𝐻 be two groups, let 𝜙 : 𝐻 → Aut(𝑁 ) be a group
homomorphism, where Aut(𝑁 ) denotes the group of automorphisms of 𝑁 under composition, and let
𝜙ℎ = 𝜙(ℎ). Then, the semidirect product 𝑁 ⋊𝜙 𝐻 is defined as the set of pairs (𝑛, ℎ) ∈ 𝑁 × 𝐻 with
multiplication

                     (𝑛, ℎ) · (𝑛′ , ℎ′ ) = 𝑛𝜙ℎ (𝑛′ ), ℎℎ′            ∀𝑛, 𝑛′ ∈ 𝑁,      ℎ, ℎ′ ∈ 𝐻.   (A9)
                                         (︀                 )︀


We abbreviate (𝑛, 𝑒𝐻 ) by 𝑛 and (𝑒𝑁 , ℎ) by ℎ. Then 𝑛ℎ = (𝑛, ℎ), so every element is written uniquely
with its 𝑁 -factor first. Written with this convention, a direct calculation with the multiplication law
Eq. (A9) gives

                              ℎ𝑛 = (𝑒𝑁 , ℎ)(𝑛, 𝑒𝐻 ) = (𝜙ℎ (𝑛), ℎ) = 𝜙ℎ (𝑛)ℎ,

or equivalently,

                                               𝜙ℎ (𝑛) = ℎ𝑛ℎ−1 .

   As the lifted product construction applies to any finite group 𝐺, we next review the necessary
background on group algebras and their representations.

Definition 8 (Group ring and regular representations). Let 𝐺 be a finite group. The group algebra
                                                   ⎧                              ⎫
                                                   ⎨ ∑︁                           ⎬
                                   ℛ = F2 [𝐺] =                  𝛼𝑔 𝑔 : 𝛼𝑔 ∈ F2                    (A10)
                                                   ⎩                              ⎭
                                                       𝑔∈𝐺

                                                                                                     |𝐺|
is the F2 -vector space with basis 𝐺 and multiplication induced by the group law. We write b(𝑔) ∈ F2
for the standard basis vector corresponding to 𝑔 ∈ 𝐺. By linearity, a ring element 𝑎 = 𝑔 𝛼𝑔 𝑔 ∈ ℛ
                                                                                         ∑︀

corresponds to the binary vector

                                              b(𝑎) =                                               (A11)
                                                       ∑︁
                                                                 𝛼𝑔 b(𝑔).
                                                       𝑔∈𝐺

The left regular representation and right regular representation are first defined on group elements by

                      𝐿(𝑔)b(ℎ) = b(𝑔ℎ),           𝑅(𝑔)b(ℎ) = b(ℎ𝑔 −1 ),               𝑔, ℎ ∈ 𝐺,    (A12)

and then extended linearly to all 𝑎 ∈ ℛ. When the context is clear, we suppress the b(·) notation and
write, for example, 𝑅(𝑔)ℎ = ℎ𝑔 −1 , meaning 𝑅(𝑔)b(ℎ) = b(ℎ𝑔 −1 ). In coordinates, for 𝑎 = 𝑔 𝛼𝑔 𝑔,
                                                                                              ∑︀

these matrices are

                         𝐿(𝑎)𝑦,𝑥 = 𝛼𝑦𝑥−1 ,         𝑅(𝑎)𝑦,𝑥 = 𝛼𝑦−1 𝑥 ,             𝑥, 𝑦 ∈ 𝐺.        (A13)
                                                                                                                 24


   The left and right regular representations commute:

                                  𝐿(𝑎)𝑅(𝑏) = 𝑅(𝑏)𝐿(𝑎)                  ∀ 𝑎, 𝑏 ∈ ℛ.                           (A14)

When we have a matrix 𝑀 defined over ℛ, we can expand it to a binary matrix by replacing each entry
with its corresponding left or right regular representation. We will overload notation and denote the
binary matrix obtained by replacing each entry of 𝑀 with its left (resp. right) regular representation
as 𝐿(𝑀 ) (resp. 𝑅(𝑀 )). Similarly, if we have a vector 𝑣 where each entry is a ring element, we write
b(𝑣) for the binary vector obtained by replacing each entry of 𝑣 with its corresponding binary vector.

Definition 9 (Involution and conjugate transpose). For 𝑎 =                  𝑔 𝛼𝑔 𝑔 ∈ ℛ, its involution is defined as
                                                                          ∑︀


                                                 𝑎* =         𝛼𝑔 𝑔 −1 .                                      (A15)
                                                        ∑︁

                                                        𝑔∈𝐺

For a matrix 𝑀 over ℛ, its conjugate transpose 𝑀 * is defined entrywise as

                                                 (𝑀 * )𝑖𝑗 = (𝑀𝑗𝑖 )* .                                        (A16)

   This definition of conjugate transpose over the ring is compatible with the ordinary binary trans-
pose after expansion:

                              𝐿(𝑀 * ) = 𝐿(𝑀 )𝑇          and        𝑅(𝑀 * ) = 𝑅(𝑀 )𝑇 .                        (A17)

To see this, first take a single group element 𝑔 ∈ 𝐺. The matrices 𝐿(𝑔) and 𝑅(𝑔) are permutation
matrices, and transposing a permutation matrix gives the inverse permutation. Hence 𝐿(𝑔)𝑇 = 𝐿(𝑔 −1 )
and 𝑅(𝑔)𝑇 = 𝑅(𝑔 −1 ). Extending linearly gives, for 𝑎 = 𝑔 𝛼𝑔 𝑔,
                                                       ∑︀


                            𝐿(𝑎)𝑇 =         𝛼𝑔 𝐿(𝑔)𝑇 =             𝛼𝑔 𝐿(𝑔 −1 ) = 𝐿(𝑎* ),                     (A18)
                                      ∑︁                      ∑︁

                                       𝑔                       𝑔

and the same argument applies to 𝑅(·). For matrices over ℛ, the ordinary binary transpose also
swaps block positions, which is exactly the swap in (𝑀 * )𝑖𝑗 = (𝑀𝑗𝑖 )* .
   We now have all the necessary tools to define the lifted product code construction.


                                       3.        Lifted product codes

    Lifted product codes generalize hypergraph product codes through a richer internal structure
afforded by the regular representations of a group algebra. Instead of taking the product of two
ordinary binary classical codes, we allow the parity-check matrices to have entries in a group algebra,
and each group algebra entry expands to a structured binary block via its left and right regular
representations. The left and right regular representations are the two commuting actions that make
this expansion compatible with the CSS commutation condition.

Definition 10 (Lifted product code). Let ℛ = F2 [𝐺] for a finite group 𝐺, and let 𝐴 ∈ ℛ𝑟1 ×𝑐1 and
𝐵 ∈ ℛ𝑟2 ×𝑐2 . The lifted product code LP(𝐴, 𝐵) is the CSS code on 𝑛 = |𝐺| (𝑐1 𝑐2 + 𝑟1 𝑟2 ) physical qubits
with parity-check matrices given by

                                   𝐻𝑋 = 𝐿(𝐴 ⊗ 𝐼𝑐2 ) ⃒ 𝑅(𝐼𝑟1 ⊗ 𝐵 * ) ,                                        (A19)
                                            [︀                 ⃒               ]︀

                                   𝐻𝑍 = 𝑅(𝐼𝑐1 ⊗ 𝐵) ⃒ 𝐿(𝐴* ⊗ 𝐼𝑟2 ) .                                          (A20)
                                            [︀     ⃒             ]︀


   The lifted product construction can be viewed as a generalization of the hypergraph product
construction (A1)–(A2) where the tensor products and transposes are interpreted over the group
algebra ℛ = F2 [𝐺] and then expanded to binary matrices using the regular representations of ℛ. We
can verify that the CSS commutation condition holds for LP codes.
                                                                                                         25


Remark 1 (Relation to balanced product codes). Lifted product codes are a special case of the
balanced product construction of [41]. The tensor product over ℛ = F2 [𝐺] in (A49) is the balanced
product of two classical chain complexes, in which the diagonal action of 𝐺 is quotiented out via the
balancing relation. When the chain groups are free modules, as in this case, the balanced and lifted
products coincide.
Proposition 1 (CSS condition for LP codes). The parity-check matrices 𝐻𝑋 and 𝐻𝑍 defined in
(A19)–(A20) satisfy
                                                      𝐻𝑋 𝐻𝑍𝑇 = 0.
Proof. From Definition 10, we have
                      𝐻𝑋 𝐻𝑍𝑇 = 𝐿(𝐴 ⊗ 𝐼𝑐2 ) 𝑅(𝐼𝑐1 ⊗ 𝐵)𝑇 + 𝑅(𝐼𝑟1 ⊗ 𝐵 * ) 𝐿(𝐴* ⊗ 𝐼𝑟2 )𝑇 .                (A21)
Using the identities
                                     𝐿(𝑀 )𝑇 = 𝐿(𝑀 * ),         𝑅(𝑀 )𝑇 = 𝑅(𝑀 * )
followed by the identities
                               (𝑀 ⊗ 𝐼)* = 𝑀 * ⊗ 𝐼,             (𝐼 ⊗ 𝑁 )* = 𝐼 ⊗ 𝑁 * ,
(A21) simplifies to
                       𝐻𝑋 𝐻𝑍𝑇 = 𝐿(𝐴 ⊗ 𝐼𝑐2 ) 𝑅(𝐼𝑐1 ⊗ 𝐵 * ) + 𝑅(𝐼𝑟1 ⊗ 𝐵 * ) 𝐿(𝐴 ⊗ 𝐼𝑟2 )                 (A22)
                                     ⏟            ⏞                  ⏟             ⏞
                                                𝑇1                               𝑇2
                               =0                                                                     (A23)
where we have obtained the last equality by using the identity (𝐴 ⊗ 𝐵)(𝐶 ⊗ 𝐷) = 𝐴𝐶 ⊗ 𝐵𝐷 and
the fact that the left and right regular representations commute to show that 𝑇1 = 𝑇2 and hence
𝑇1 + 𝑇2 = 0 over F2 .


   The number of physical qubits in the lifted product code is
                                               𝑛 = |𝐺| (𝑐1 𝑐2 + 𝑟1 𝑟2 ),                              (A24)
since the two qubit blocks have sizes 𝑐1 𝑐2 and 𝑟1 𝑟2 over the group algebra, and each group-ring
coordinate expands to |𝐺| binary coordinates. The number of logical qubits can always be computed
from the expanded binary check matrices as
                                         𝑘 = 𝑛 − rankF2 (𝐻𝑋 ) − rankF2 (𝐻𝑍 )                          (A25)
which leads to the elementary lower bound
                        𝑘 ≥ |𝐺| 𝑐1 𝑐2 + 𝑟1 𝑟2 − 𝑟1 𝑐2 − 𝑐1 𝑟2 = |𝐺| (𝑐1 − 𝑟1 )(𝑐2 − 𝑟2 ).             (A26)
                                (︀                              )︀

In later sections when we prove distance bounds and construct a canonical logical basis for lifted
product codes, it will be convenient to have a dictionary that lets us translate between working over
the ring ℛ and working over F2 . We first fix our conventions. Over ℛ, a matrix acts on column
vectors from the left, as in 𝑀 𝑢, and on row vectors from the right, as in 𝑣𝑀 ; matrix products over ℛ
multiply entries in the order written and are associative. In binary coordinates, we will always treat
b(𝑣) as a column vector, regardless of whether 𝑣 is a row or a column over ℛ.
    Furthermore, because the blocks of the parity-check matrices in Definition 10 are Kronecker prod-
ucts over ℛ, the vectors they act on are naturally indexed by pairs, and it will be convenient to
                                                              ′                    ′
view them as flattened matrices over ℛ. For 𝑉 ∈ ℛ𝑚×𝑚 , let vec(𝑉 ) ∈ ℛ𝑚𝑚 denote the row-major
flattening of 𝑉 , which lists the entries of the first row, then those of the second row, and so on:
                       vec(𝑉 )(𝑖−1)𝑚′ +𝑗 = 𝑉𝑖𝑗 ,        𝑖 ∈ {1, . . . , 𝑚},   𝑗 ∈ {1, . . . , 𝑚′ }.   (A27)
The following proposition collects all the compatibility properties between the ring and binary de-
scriptions that we will need.
                                                                                                              26


Proposition 2. Let 𝑀 ∈ ℛ𝑟×𝑐 and 𝑁 ∈ ℛ𝑟 ×𝑐 be matrices over ℛ. The map b : ℛ𝑐 → F2
                                                      ′   ′                                           𝑐|𝐺|
                                                                                                             is a
bijection that satisfies the following properties:
  (i) (Matrix-vector products) For a column vector 𝑢 ∈ ℛ𝑐 and a row vector 𝑣 ∈ ℛ1×𝑐 ,

                                𝐿(𝑀 ) b(𝑢) = b(𝑀 𝑢)           and     𝑅(𝑀 ) b(𝑣) = b(𝑣𝑀 * ).            (A28)
                                                                                               ′
 (ii) (Kronecker products with an identity factor) For 𝑉 ∈ ℛ𝑐×𝑚 and 𝑊 ∈ ℛ𝑚×𝑐 ,

        𝐿(𝑀 ⊗ 𝐼𝑚 ) b(vec(𝑉 )) = b(vec(𝑀 𝑉 ))              and    𝑅(𝐼𝑚 ⊗ 𝑁 ) b(vec(𝑊 )) = b(vec(𝑊 𝑁 * )). (A29)

Proof. Using Definitions 8 and 9, it is straightforward to verify that b is a bijection that satisfies the
stated properties.


                           4.    Mitten codes and structured mitten codes

   In Definition 4, we defined mitten codes, which are a class of LP codes with 1 × 2 base matrices
and non-abelian 𝐺 with further condition that 𝐿(𝑎1 ) and 𝑅(𝑏1 ) being full-rank. Here we single out
a structured subfamily whose extra algebraic symmetry lets the syndrome-extraction (SE) cycle run
ideally twice as fast with 2 AODs (Appendix H.5). Recall the involution 𝑎* = 𝑔 𝛼𝑔 𝑔 −1 of Definition 8.
                                                                             ∑︀

A ring element is self-adjoint if 𝑎* = 𝑎. The structured mitten codes are mitten codes in which every
base matrix entry is self-adjoint,

                                  𝑎*0 = 𝑎0 ,   𝑎*1 = 𝑎1 ,       𝑏*0 = 𝑏0 ,   𝑏*1 = 𝑏1 .                 (A30)

For specific parameters of both mitten and structured mitten codes studied in this paper, see Section K
and Table XIII.


                                          5.    Homological algebra

   We will reformulate the lifted product construction in the language of homological algebra. This
way of constructing lifted product codes is akin to the machinery of the more general balanced product
construction of Ref. [41]. This perspective provides a systematic route to construct quantum codes
from classical codes via tensor products of chain complexes and will later allow us to better understand
the structure of the lifted product codes.
   The starting observation is to note that a classical linear code can be described in terms of the
homology of a two-term chain complex:
                                                                𝜕=𝐻
                                               𝐶∙ :       𝐶1 −−−→ 𝐶0 ,                                  (A31)

where 𝐶1 = F𝑐2 is the vector space of bits, 𝐶0 = F𝑟2 is the vector space of parity checks, and the
boundary map 𝜕 : 𝐶1 → 𝐶0 is given by matrix multiplication by the parity-check matrix 𝐻 ∈
 2 . The classical linear code with parity-check matrix 𝐻 is precisely the first homology group
F𝑟×𝑐
𝐻1 (𝒞(𝜕)) = ker 𝜕 = ker 𝐻, i.e. the set of vectors that satisfy all parity checks. The zeroth homology
group 𝐻0 (𝒞(𝜕)) = 𝐶0 / im 𝜕 = F𝑟2 / im 𝐻 is nontrivial whenever the parity check matrix 𝐻 has linearly
dependent rows.
   One can view a CSS code as two separate classical codes with parity-check matrices 𝐻𝑋 and 𝐻𝑍
that must be compatible in the sense that they satisfy 𝐻𝑋 𝐻𝑍𝑇 = 0. The natural algebraic object that
captures this compatibility is a three-term chain complex
                                               𝜕2 =𝐻 𝑇
                                                    𝐶1 −−1−−−𝑋
                                                                 𝜕 =𝐻
                                                  𝑍
                                          𝐶2 −−−−−→          → 𝐶0                                       (A32)

where 𝐶2 , 𝐶1 , and 𝐶0 are F2 -vector spaces whose basis elements label the 𝑍 checks, qubits, and
𝑋 checks respectively. Qubits are placed on 𝐶1 , and the parity-check matrices are 𝐻𝑋 = 𝜕1 and
                                                                                                                        27


𝐻𝑍 = 𝜕2𝑇 . The CSS commutation condition 𝐻𝑋 𝐻𝑍𝑇 = 0 is then simply the statement that 𝜕1 𝜕2 = 0,
i.e. that (A32) is indeed a chain complex. X-stabilizers are rows of 𝜕1 , while Z-stabilizers are columns
of 𝜕2 (equivalently, rows of 𝜕2𝑇 ). A Z-type logical operator commutes with all the 𝑋-stabilizers but is
not itself a 𝑍-stabilizer. Such an operator can be represented as a vector 𝑧 ∈ 𝐶1 that is in the kernel
of 𝜕1 = 𝐻𝑋 but is not in the image of 𝜕2 = 𝐻𝑍𝑇 . Hence Z-type logical operators are represented by
nontrivial classes in the first homology group

                                                  𝐻1 = ker 𝜕1 / im 𝜕2 .                                              (A33)

Dually, X-type logical operators are represented by the first cohomology group 𝐻 1 = ker 𝜕2𝑇 / im 𝜕1𝑇 .
   The question, then, is how to produce a three-term chain complex that corresponds to a CSS code
from classical input data in the form of two-term chain complexes. There is a standard algebraic
construction that does exactly this: the tensor product of chain complexes.

Definition 11 (Tensor product of chain complexes). Given two two-term chain complexes over F2 -
valued vector spaces,
                                              𝜕                                               𝜕
                              𝐶∙𝐴 : 𝐶1𝐴 −−𝐴→ 𝐶0𝐴 ,                   𝐶∙𝐵 : 𝐶1𝐵 −−𝐵→ 𝐶0𝐵 ,                            (A34)

their tensor-product complex 𝐶∙𝐴 ⊗ 𝐶∙𝐵 is the three-term complex

                                  2
                                  𝜕                                                             1 𝜕
                      𝐶1𝐴 ⊗ 𝐶1𝐵 −−→ 𝐶1𝐴 ⊗ 𝐶0𝐵 ⊕ 𝐶0𝐴 ⊗ 𝐶1𝐵                                         𝐶0𝐴 ⊗ 𝐶0𝐵 ,        (A35)
                                         (︀              )︀     (︀                       )︀
                                                                                              −−→

with boundary maps
                                   [︃             ]︃
                                  𝐼 ⊗ 𝜕𝐵
                             𝜕2 =                             𝜕1 = 𝜕𝐴 ⊗ 𝐼 ⃒ 𝐼 ⊗ 𝜕𝐵 .                                 (A36)
                                                                      [︀                  ⃒           ]︀
                                         ,
                                  𝜕𝐴 ⊗ 𝐼

   One can verify directly that 𝜕1 𝜕2 = 0:

               𝜕1 𝜕2 = (𝜕𝐴 ⊗ 𝐼)(𝐼 ⊗ 𝜕𝐵 ) + (𝐼 ⊗ 𝜕𝐵 )(𝜕𝐴 ⊗ 𝐼) = 𝜕𝐴 ⊗ 𝜕𝐵 + 𝜕𝐴 ⊗ 𝜕𝐵 = 0,                                (A37)

so the tensor-product complex is indeed a chain complex, and the resulting CSS code automatically
satisfies the commutation condition. To construct the hypergraph product code from classical codes
with parity-check matrices 𝐴 and 𝐵, we take the tensor product of the chain complex 𝐶∙𝐴 with the
dual of 𝐶∙𝐵 .

Definition 12 (Dual of a chain complex). Let 𝐶∙ : F𝑐2 −−→ F𝑟2 be a two-term chain complex, where
                                                                                𝐻

the chain groups are equipped with their standard bases. We define its dual complex as

                                                   𝐶^∙ : F𝑟2 −−−→ F𝑐2 .
                                                              𝐻  𝑇
                                                                                                                     (A38)

   The tensor product 𝐶∙𝐴 ⊗ 𝐶^∙𝐵 then has middle chain group (F𝑐21 ⊗ F𝑐22 ) ⊕ (F𝑟21 ⊗ F𝑟22 ), and the maps
𝜕1 and 𝜕2𝑇 reproduce (A1)–(A2). The dimension of the code follows from the Künneth theorem.

Theorem 3 (Künneth formula [108]). Let 𝐶∙ and 𝐷∙ be chain complexes of finite-dimensional F2 -
vector spaces. Then the homology of their tensor product satisfies

                                𝐻𝑛 (𝐶∙ ⊗ 𝐷∙ ) ∼                  𝐻𝑖 (𝐶∙ ) ⊗ 𝐻𝑗 (𝐷∙ ).                                (A39)
                                                         ⨁︁
                                              =
                                                        𝑖+𝑗=𝑛


   For the hypergraph product, we apply this theorem to 𝐶∙𝐴 ⊗ 𝐶^∙𝐵 . Since the logical qubits are
represented by the middle homology group, we obtain

                                     = 𝐻1 (𝐶∙𝐴 ) ⊗ 𝐻0 (𝐶^∙𝐵 ) ⊕ 𝐻0 (𝐶∙𝐴 ) ⊗ 𝐻1 (𝐶^∙𝐵 ) .
                    𝐻1 (𝐶∙𝐴 ⊗ 𝐶^∙𝐵 ) ∼                                                                               (A40)
                                        (︀                                 )︀       (︀                          )︀
                                                                                                   28


Taking dimensions gives the usual formula

                                    𝑘 = ℎ(𝐴)ℎ(𝐵) + ℎ⊥ (𝐴)ℎ⊥ (𝐵),                                (A41)

where

                             ℎ(𝐴) = dim ker 𝐴,            ℎ⊥ (𝐴) = dim ker 𝐴𝑇 ,                 (A42)

and similarly for 𝐵.
    To pass from the hypergraph product to the lifted product, we replace F2 -vector spaces by modules
over the ring ℛ = F2 [𝐺]. A module can be thought of as generalizing the notion of a vector space by
allowing scalars to come from a ring instead of a field.

Definition 13 (ℛ-modules). Let ℛ be a ring. A left ℛ-module is an abelian group 𝑀 equipped with
a scalar multiplication

                                   ℛ × 𝑀 → 𝑀,              (𝑎, 𝑚) ↦→ 𝑎𝑚,

satisfying the following axioms for all 𝑎, 𝑏 ∈ ℛ and all 𝑚, 𝑛 ∈ 𝑀 :

             𝑎(𝑚 + 𝑛) = 𝑎𝑚 + 𝑎𝑛,                  (left distributivity over module addition)
             (𝑎 + 𝑏)𝑚 = 𝑎𝑚 + 𝑏𝑚,                  (left distributivity over ring addition)
                (𝑎𝑏)𝑚 = 𝑎(𝑏𝑚),                    (associativity)
                   1𝑚 = 𝑚.                        (identity)

A right ℛ-module is defined similarly, except that scalar multiplication acts on the right:

                                   𝑀 × ℛ → 𝑀,              (𝑚, 𝑎) ↦→ 𝑚𝑎.

The corresponding axioms are

            (𝑚 + 𝑛)𝑎 = 𝑚𝑎 + 𝑛𝑎,                  (right distributivity over module addition)
             𝑚(𝑎 + 𝑏) = 𝑚𝑎 + 𝑚𝑏,                 (right distributivity over ring addition)
               (𝑚𝑎)𝑏 = 𝑚(𝑎𝑏),                    (associativity)
                  𝑚1 = 𝑚.                        (identity)

When ℛ is commutative, left and right ℛ-modules are equivalent.

Remark 2. The distinction between left and right modules is not really about which side of 𝑚 we write
the scalar. We could just as easily write a right action on the left, as (𝑎, 𝑚) ↦→ 𝑎𝑚, and define the
associativity axiom by (𝑎𝑏)𝑚 = 𝑏(𝑎𝑚) instead of (𝑎𝑏)𝑚 = 𝑎(𝑏𝑚). This difference in the associativity
axiom is the only true distinguishing feature between left and right modules.

Example 2. The basic example is the free module ℛ𝑛 . Its elements are vectors

                                            𝑟1
                                            ⎛     ⎞
                                           ⎜ .. ⎟
                                       𝑣 = ⎝ . ⎠,            𝑟𝑖 ∈ ℛ.
                                             𝑟𝑛

When ℛ𝑛 is regarded as a left ℛ-module, we use the notation ℛ ℛ𝑛 and scalars 𝑎 ∈ ℛ act entrywise
on the left:

                                                  𝑎𝑟1
                                                      ⎛     ⎞
                                                 ⎜ .. ⎟
                                            𝑎𝑣 = ⎝ . ⎠ .
                                                      𝑎𝑟𝑛
                                                                                                     29


When ℛ𝑛 is regarded as a right ℛ-module, we use the notation ℛ𝑛ℛ and scalars 𝑎 ∈ ℛ act entrywise
on the right:

                                                       𝑟1 𝑎
                                                       ⎛       ⎞
                                                      ⎜ .. ⎟
                                                 𝑣𝑎 = ⎝ . ⎠ .
                                                            𝑟𝑛 𝑎

Sometimes it will also be convenient for us to view the elements of ℛ ℛ𝑛 as row vectors.

    We constructed the hypergraph product code by taking the tensor product of two chain com-
plexes defined over F2 -vector spaces. This was defined degree-by-degree from tensor products of the
underlying vector spaces, as in (A35). For lifted product codes, we use the same product-complex
construction, but with vector spaces replaced by modules over the group algebra ℛ = F2 [𝐺].
    To do this, we need the tensor product of modules over ℛ. Let 𝐾 be a right ℛ-module and let 𝐿 be
a left ℛ-module. Tensoring over ℛ means that scalars from ℛ should act as scalars in the combined
object, rather than as data belonging separately to one factor or the other. Therefore, for 𝑘 ∈ 𝐾,
ℓ ∈ 𝐿, and 𝑎 ∈ ℛ, multiplying 𝑘 by 𝑎 on the right should be identified with multiplying ℓ by 𝑎 on the
left. This is enforced by the balancing relation

                                              (𝑘𝑎) ⊗ ℓ = 𝑘 ⊗ (𝑎ℓ).

This relation is the module analogue of the scalar-linearity relation in the tensor product of vector
spaces that lets us move scalars across the tensor product. It only makes sense when the first module
has a right ℛ-action and the second module has a left ℛ-action.

Definition 14 (Tensor product of ℛ-modules). Let 𝐾 be a right ℛ-module and let 𝐿 be a left ℛ-
module. The tensor product 𝐾 ⊗ℛ 𝐿 is the abelian group generated by formal symbols

                                           𝑘 ⊗ ℓ,      𝑘 ∈ 𝐾, ℓ ∈ 𝐿,

subject to the relations

                                         (𝑘 + 𝑘 ′ ) ⊗ ℓ = 𝑘 ⊗ ℓ + 𝑘 ′ ⊗ ℓ,
                                         𝑘 ⊗ (ℓ + ℓ′ ) = 𝑘 ⊗ ℓ + 𝑘 ⊗ ℓ′ ,
                                            (𝑘𝑎) ⊗ ℓ = 𝑘 ⊗ (𝑎ℓ),

for all 𝑘, 𝑘 ′ ∈ 𝐾, ℓ, ℓ′ ∈ 𝐿, and 𝑎 ∈ ℛ. The symbols 𝑘 ⊗ ℓ are called pure tensors and are said to
generate 𝐾 ⊗ℛ 𝐿 since any element of 𝐾 ⊗ℛ 𝐿 can be written as a finite sum of pure tensors.

    Just as the tensor product is only well-defined between a right module and a left module, the tensor
product of chain complexes is only well-defined between a complex of right modules and a complex
of left modules.

Definition 15 (Tensor product of a right-module complex and a left-module complex). Let
                                  (︃                                              )︃
                                                      𝐾
                                                     𝜕𝑝+1          𝜕𝑝𝐾
                           𝐾∙ =    · · · −→ 𝐾𝑝+1 −−−→ 𝐾𝑝 −−→ 𝐾𝑝−1 −→ · · ·

be a chain complex of right ℛ-modules, and let
                                  (︃                                             )︃
                                                      𝐿
                                                     𝜕𝑞+1          𝜕𝑞𝐿
                           𝐿∙ =        · · · −→ 𝐿𝑞+1 −−−→ 𝐿𝑞 −−→ 𝐿𝑞−1 −→ · · ·

be a chain complex of left ℛ-modules. Their tensor product over ℛ is the chain complex of abelian
groups

                                                    𝐾∙ ⊗ℛ 𝐿∙
                                                                                                                     30


whose degree-𝑛 term is

                                         (𝐾∙ ⊗ℛ 𝐿∙ )𝑛 =
                                                                    ⨁︁
                                                                            𝐾𝑝 ⊗ℛ 𝐿𝑞 .
                                                                  𝑝+𝑞=𝑛

The boundary map

                                   𝜕𝑛 : (𝐾∙ ⊗ℛ 𝐿∙ )𝑛 −→ (𝐾∙ ⊗ℛ 𝐿∙ )𝑛−1

is defined on pure tensors by

              𝜕𝑛 (𝑘 ⊗ ℓ) = 𝜕𝑝𝐾 (𝑘) ⊗ ℓ + (−1)𝑝 𝑘 ⊗ 𝜕𝑞𝐿 (ℓ),                        𝑘 ∈ 𝐾𝑝 , ℓ ∈ 𝐿𝑞 , 𝑝 + 𝑞 = 𝑛.   (A43)

Since we will always be working over F2 , we may neglect the (−1)𝑝 factor.


                     6.    Lifted product codes: homological algebra viewpoint

    We now explain how the lifted product code construction arises from tensor products of ℛ-module
chain complexes. Let 𝐴 ∈ ℛ𝑟1 ×𝑐1 and 𝐵 ∈ ℛ𝑟2 ×𝑐2 be the classical base matrices associated with our
lifted product code. We associate to 𝐴 the two-term complex of right ℛ-modules
                                                                     𝜕 =𝐴
                                  𝐶∙𝐴 :             𝐶1𝐴 = ℛ𝑐1 −−𝐴−−→ 𝐶0𝐴 = ℛ𝑟1 .                                  (A44)

We view the elements of 𝐶1𝐴 and 𝐶0𝐴 as column vectors and the boundary map is defined by left
multiplication by 𝐴. This is compatible with the right ℛ-module structure and defines a valid homo-
morphism between right ℛ-modules: for every 𝑥 ∈ ℛ𝑐1 and 𝑟 ∈ ℛ,

                                     𝜕𝐴 (𝑥𝑟) = 𝐴(𝑥𝑟) = (𝐴𝑥)𝑟 = 𝜕𝐴 (𝑥)𝑟.                                           (A45)

   We may similarly associate a two-term complex to 𝐵. However, since the tensor product of chain
complexes is only well-defined between a right-module complex and a left-module complex, we will
instead consider the left-module complex given by the dual of 𝐶∙𝐵 :
                                                                     ^       *
                                𝐶^∙𝐵 :         𝐶^1𝐵 = ℛ ℛ𝑟2 −−𝐵−−−→ 𝐶^0𝐵 = ℛ ℛ𝑐2
                                                             𝜕 =𝐵
                                                                                                                  (A46)

Here the matrix 𝐵 * ∈ ℛ𝑐2 ×𝑟2 defines a homomorphism of free left ℛ-modules via the coordinate
formula
                                                     𝑟2

After expanding each copy of ℛ as a binary vector space of dimension |𝐺|, the middle term 𝑄1 has

binary coordinates. These correspond to the physical qubits of the lifted product code.
   We now compute the two boundary maps of 𝑄∙ which correspond to the 𝑋 and 𝑍 parity check
matrices. First consider

Using the definition of the tensor product boundary map in (A43), for 𝑥 ∈ 𝐶1𝐴 , 𝑧 ∈ 𝐶^0𝐵 , 𝑢 ∈ 𝐶0𝐴 , and

which agrees with the 𝑋-check matrix in Definition 10.

the definition for the tensor product boundary map in (A43) gives
