for 𝑥 ∈ 𝐶1𝐴 and 𝑦 ∈ 𝐶^1𝐵 . The first component applies the left-module map 𝑦 ↦→ 𝐵 * 𝑦 to the second
factor, so its binary expansion is

                                                    𝑅(𝐼𝑐1 ⊗ 𝐵 * ).

The second component applies 𝐴 to the first factor, so its binary expansion is

                                                    𝐿(𝐴 ⊗ 𝐼𝑟2 ).

Hence, as a binary matrix, 𝜕2 has the block form
                                                     [︃                ]︃
                                                    𝑅(𝐼𝑐1 ⊗ 𝐵 * )
                                               𝜕2 =
                                                    𝐿(𝐴 ⊗ 𝐼𝑟2 )

and so

               𝐻𝑍 = 𝜕2𝑇 = 𝑅(𝐼𝑐1 ⊗ 𝐵 * ) 𝑇 ⃒ 𝐿(𝐴 ⊗ 𝐼𝑟2 ) 𝑇 = 𝑅(𝐼𝑐1 ⊗ 𝐵) ⃒ 𝐿(𝐴* ⊗ 𝐼𝑟2 )
                            [︀                  ⃒                 ]︀    [︀            ⃒         ]︀


which agrees with the 𝑍-check matrix in Definition 10. Therefore, the lifted product code associated
with (𝐴, 𝐵) is exactly the CSS code corresponding to the tensor product complex

                                 𝑄∙ = 𝐶∙𝐴 ⊗ℛ 𝐶^∙𝐵 :              2      1
                                                                   𝜕         𝜕
                                                            𝑄2 −−→ 𝑄1 −−→ 𝑄0 .

The physical qubits correspond to 𝑄1 and the 𝑋 and 𝑍 stabilizers correspond to 𝑄0 and 𝑄2 respec-
tively.


                            B.      Canonical basis for lifted product codes

    In this appendix, we explain the general procedure to construct explicit low-weight representatives
for the single-qubit 𝑋 and 𝑍 logical operators of our codes. By this we mean that the representatives
satisfy the canonical commutation relation [𝑋𝑖 , 𝑍𝑗 ] = 2𝑋𝑖 𝑍𝑗 𝛿𝑖𝑗 or equivalently 𝑋𝑖 · 𝑍𝑗 = 𝛿𝑖𝑗 where the
dot product denotes the binary symplectic inner product. We refer to this collection of representatives
as a canonical basis for the logical operators.


                                                     1.   Setup

   We begin by characterizing sufficient conditions for a collection of 𝑋-type and 𝑍-type operators
to form a canonical basis and then define the square-invertibility condition which is needed for our
construction of a canonical basis for lifted product codes.


                                          a.    Canonical logical bases

   We begin by establishing a simple lemma that provides a set of sufficient conditions for identifying
a collection of 𝑋-type and 𝑍-type operators as a canonical basis for a CSS code.

Lemma 1. Let 𝐻𝑋 , 𝐻𝑍 be the parity-check matrices of a CSS code with 𝑘 logical qubits. Identify 𝑋-
type and 𝑍-type Pauli operators with their binary support vectors in F𝑛2 . Suppose we have a collection
{𝑋𝑖 }𝑘𝑖=1 and {𝑍𝑖 }𝑘𝑖=1 of such operators satisfying

                        𝑋𝑖 ∈ ker 𝐻𝑍 ,          𝑍𝑖 ∈ ker 𝐻𝑋        for every 𝑖 = 1, . . . , 𝑘,        (B1)
                                                                                                      33


and

                                               𝑋𝑖 · 𝑍𝑗 = 𝛿𝑖𝑗                                        (B2)

where the dot denotes the F2 inner product. Then 𝑋𝑖 and 𝑍𝑖 are single-qubit 𝑋 and 𝑍 logical operators
for the 𝑖-th logical qubit.
Proof. For a CSS code, an 𝑋-type operator commutes with all 𝑍-stabilizers precisely when its binary
support lies in ker 𝐻𝑍 . To show that 𝑋𝑖 is a nontrivial logical operator, it remains to show that it is
not an 𝑋-stabilizer, i.e. that 𝑋𝑖 ∈     / row(𝐻𝑋 ).
   Suppose, for contradiction, that 𝑋𝑖 ∈ row(𝐻𝑋 ). Let ℎ1 , . . . , ℎ𝑚 be the rows of 𝐻𝑋 . Then there
exist coefficients 𝑐1 , . . . , 𝑐𝑚 ∈ F2 such that
                                                       𝑚
                                               𝑋𝑖 =
                                                       ∑︁
                                                              𝑐𝑎 ℎ𝑎 .
                                                       𝑎=1

Since 𝑍𝑗 ∈ ker 𝐻𝑋 , we have

                                ℎ𝑎 · 𝑍 𝑗 = 0         for every 𝑎 = 1, . . . , 𝑚.

Hence we have
                                                     𝑚
                                     𝑋𝑖 · 𝑍𝑗 =             𝑐𝑎 (ℎ𝑎 · 𝑍𝑗 ) = 0
                                                     ∑︁

                                                     𝑎=1

for all 𝑍𝑗 . This contradicts the assumption that 𝑋𝑖 · 𝑍𝑗 = 𝛿𝑖𝑗 , so we conclude that 𝑋𝑖 ∈/ row(𝐻𝑋 ),
and hence 𝑋𝑖 is a nontrivial logical operator. A similar argument shows that all the 𝑍𝑖 are nontrivial
logical operators as well. Since they satisfy the canonical commutation relation (B2), they are the
single-qubit 𝑋 and 𝑍 logical operators for the 𝑘 logical qubits.

    We take the most general definition of a canonical logical basis to be a collection of 𝑋-type and
𝑍-type representatives satisfying the conditions of Lemma 1. The canonical basis we will construct
for non-abelian lifted product codes enjoys three additional properties beyond this pairing: its repre-
sentatives are built directly from classical codewords of the base matrices, the logical operators within
a basis are related by group action, and the supports of conjugate pairs intersect on exactly one data
qubit.


                                    b.   Square-invertibility condition

   Let ℛ = F2 [𝐺], and let

                                     𝐴 ∈ ℛ𝑟1 ×𝑐1 ,            𝐵 ∈ ℛ𝑟2 ×𝑐2 ,

with 𝑟1 < 𝑐1 and 𝑟2 < 𝑐2 , be the classical base matrices defining the lifted product code LP(𝐴, 𝐵).
The following definition captures the assumptions we will make about the structure of 𝐴 and 𝐵.
Definition 16 (Square invertibility condition). Let 𝑀 ∈ ℛ𝑟×𝑐 with 𝑟 < 𝑐. We say that 𝑀 satisfies
the square invertibility condition with respect to a chosen binary expansion of ℛ if, after possibly
reordering its columns, 𝑀 can be written as

                                         𝑀 = 𝑀free | 𝑀pivot ,
                                                [︀                      ]︀


where

                                 𝑀free ∈ ℛ𝑟×(𝑐−𝑟) ,             𝑀pivot ∈ ℛ𝑟×𝑟 ,

and the binary expansion of 𝑀pivot is an invertible matrix over F2 . We call the first 𝑐 − 𝑟 columns the
free columns and the last 𝑟 columns the pivot columns.
                                                                                                       34


   After possibly reordering columns, we can fix decompositions

                             𝐴 = 𝐴free | 𝐴pivot ,                 𝐵 = 𝐵free | 𝐵pivot ,               (B3)
                                  [︀                 ]︀                 [︀              ]︀


where

                                 𝐴pivot ∈ ℛ𝑟1 ×𝑟1 ,                𝐵pivot ∈ ℛ𝑟2 ×𝑟2 ,

and 𝐿(𝐴pivot ) and 𝑅(𝐵pivot ) are invertible binary matrices. Letting

                                       𝑓𝐴 := 𝑐1 − 𝑟1 ,            𝑓𝐵 := 𝑐2 − 𝑟2 ,

the decompositions in (B3) induce coordinate splittings

                               ℛ𝑐1 = ℛ𝑓𝐴 ⊕ ℛ𝑟1 ,                   ℛ𝑐2 = ℛ𝑓𝐵 ⊕ ℛ𝑟2 .

We refer to the first summand as the free coordinates and to the second summand as the pivot
coordinates. Throughout this section, we will assume that 𝐴 satisfies the square invertibility condi-
tion (Definition 16) with respect to the left regular representation, and that 𝐵 satisfies the square
invertibility condition with respect to the right regular representation.


                                               2.    Main result

    We now state the main result of this section: an explicit canonical logical basis for the lifted
product code LP(𝐴, 𝐵). The construction of this basis exploits the product structure of the code;
each logical representative is derived from a codeword of one of the classical base codes. Logical 𝑋
operators arise from codewords of 𝐵 and logical 𝑍 operators arise from codewords of 𝐴. The key step
is choosing suitable bases for the two classical codes.
    The square invertibility condition (Definition 16) ensures that 𝐴 and 𝐵 admit bases in which each
basis codeword is supported on exactly one of the free coordinates, a distinct one for each codeword.
After padding with zeros, these classical basis vectors become logical 𝑋 and 𝑍 representatives that
pair up canonically – within each pair, the supports of 𝑋 and 𝑍 overlap on exactly one physical qubit
(hence the operators anticommute), while operators from different pairs have disjoint support (and
therefore commute).
    The basis is built from distinguished codewords of the two classical factor codes, which we now
define. For 1 ≤ 𝛼 ≤ 𝑓𝐴 and 𝑔 ∈ 𝐺, let 𝜖𝛼𝐴 (𝑔) ∈ ℛ𝑓𝐴 denote the vector with 𝑔 in the 𝛼-th coordinate
and zero elsewhere. Similarly, for 1 ≤ 𝛽 ≤ 𝑓𝐵 , let 𝜖𝛽𝐵 (𝑔) ∈ ℛ𝑓𝐵 denote the vector with 𝑔 in the 𝛽-th
coordinate and zero elsewhere. We first define the kernel vectors. Let
                                                          (︁                       )︁
                                        𝛼,𝑔                           𝛽,𝑔
                                       𝑣𝐴   ∈ ℛ𝑐1              resp. 𝑣𝐵   ∈ ℛ𝑐2

be the unique element of ker 𝐿(𝐴) (resp. ker 𝑅(𝐵)) whose free part is 𝜖𝛼𝐴 (𝑔) (resp. 𝜖𝛽𝐵 (𝑔)); its pivot
part is uniquely determined because 𝐿(𝐴pivot ) (resp. 𝑅(𝐵pivot )) is invertible (Lemma 2). Likewise,
let
                                                          (︁                       )︁
                                        𝛼,𝑔                           𝛽,𝑔
                                       𝑤𝐴   ∈ ℛ𝑐1              resp. 𝑤𝐵   ∈ ℛ𝑐2

be the vector whose free part is 𝜖𝛼𝐴 (𝑔) (resp. 𝜖𝛽𝐵 (𝑔)) and whose pivot part is zero; it is the unique
representative of its class in coker 𝐿(𝐴* ) (resp. coker 𝑅(𝐵 * )) supported only on the free coordinates
(Lemma 2). The canonical logical basis for LP(𝐴, 𝐵) is characterized by the following theorem.
Theorem 4 (Canonical basis of logical operators). Let 𝐴 ∈ ℛ𝑟1 ×𝑐1 and 𝐵 ∈ ℛ𝑟2 ×𝑐2 satisfy the
square invertibility condition (Definition 16) with respect to the left and right regular representations,
respectively. For 1 ≤ 𝛼 ≤ 𝑓𝐴 , 1 ≤ 𝛽 ≤ 𝑓𝐵 , and 𝑔 ∈ 𝐺, define
                                                (︁                            )︁
                                        𝑔         𝛼,𝑔     𝛽,𝑒𝐺
                                       𝑍𝛼,𝛽 := b(𝑣𝐴   ⊗ℛ 𝑤𝐵    ), 0 ,                                (B4)
                                                                                                                                    35


and
                                                     (︁                                  )︁
                                        𝑔         𝛼,𝑔     𝛽,𝑒𝐺
                                       𝑋𝛼,𝛽 := b(𝑤𝐴   ⊗ℛ 𝑣𝐵    ), 0 .                                                              (B5)

These operators form a canonical logical basis for the 𝑘 = 𝑓𝐴 𝑓𝐵 |𝐺| logical qubits of LP(𝐴, 𝐵).

Remark 3. For our mitten codes we have 𝑓𝐴 = 𝑓𝐵 = 1, so we may drop the 𝛼 and 𝛽 subscripts and
simply label the 𝑋 and 𝑍 logical operators by the group elements of 𝐺. The logical operators of one
basis are all related to each other via group action. For example, for 𝑋, we have
                                                     𝑔
                                           𝑋 𝑔 = (b(𝑤𝐴     𝑒
                                                       ⊗ℛ 𝑣𝐵 ), 0)
                                                    = (b(𝑔 𝑤𝐴
                                                            𝑒     𝑒
                                                              ⊗ℛ 𝑣𝐵 ), 0)                                                          (B6)
                                                    =𝑔·𝑋 .       𝑒


where the action 𝑔· is defined via the last equality. A similar relationship holds for the 𝑍 logicals, with
the group element acting by right multiplication instead: 𝑍 𝑔 = 𝑍 𝑒 · 𝑔.

   The rest of this section is devoted to proving Theorem 4. We first introduce the four binary vector
spaces from which the logical operators will be constructed. For the remainder of this section, we will
                                          |𝐺|𝑚
identify ℛ𝑚 with its binary expansion F2 . Define the kernels
                                                     |𝐺|𝑐1                                                |𝐺|𝑐2
                         𝑉𝐴 := ker 𝐿(𝐴) ⊆ F2                 ,         𝑉𝐵 := ker 𝑅(𝐵) ⊆ F2                                         (B7)

and the cokernels
                               |𝐺|𝑐1                                                                       |𝐺|𝑐2
       𝑊𝐴 := coker 𝐿(𝐴* ) = F2         / im 𝐿(𝐴* ),                  𝑊𝐵 := coker 𝑅(𝐵 * ) = F2                      / im 𝑅(𝐵 * ).   (B8)

Under the square invertibility condition, the elements of these four spaces can be expressed in a
particularly simple form.

Lemma 2. Assume 𝐴 and 𝐵 satisfy the square invertibility condition. Then:

   1. every element of 𝑉𝐴 and 𝑉𝐵 is uniquely determined by its free coordinates;

   2. every equivalence class in 𝑊𝐴 and 𝑊𝐵 has a unique representative supported only on the free
      coordinates.

Proof. We prove the two statements for 𝐴. The proof for 𝐵 is identical, with left representations
replaced by right representations.
   First, consider the kernel. Using the decomposition
                                                |𝐺|𝑐1            |𝐺|𝑓𝐴           |𝐺|𝑟1
                                               F2         = F2           ⊕ F2

into free and pivot coordinates, write
                                       (︃ )︃
                                        𝑥                            |𝐺|𝑓𝐴                    |𝐺|𝑟1
                               𝑣=         ,               𝑥 ∈ F2             ,      𝑦 ∈ F2            .
                                        𝑦

Then 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉𝐴 = ker 𝐿(𝐴) means

                                         𝐿(𝐴free )𝑥 + 𝐿(𝐴pivot )𝑦 = 0.

Since 𝐿(𝐴pivot ) is invertible over F2 , the pivot part 𝑦 is uniquely determined by the free part 𝑥. Hence,
every element of 𝑉𝐴 is uniquely determined by its free coordinates.
   Next, consider the cokernel
                                                           |𝐺|𝑐1
                                           𝑊𝐴 = F2                   / im 𝐿(𝐴* ).
                                                                                                                       36


Let
                                (︃ )︃

                                  𝑦

We want to replace 𝑤 by an equivalent representative whose pivot part is zero. Since representatives
in the same equivalence class may differ by an element of im 𝐿(𝐴* ), we may add a vector of the form
                                                                     |𝐺|𝑟1
                                             𝐿(𝐴* )𝑢,           𝑢 ∈ F2        .

Hence,
                                                           (︃                     )︃

Because 𝐿(𝐴pivot ) is invertible, so is 𝐿(𝐴*pivot ), and so there is a unique 𝑢 ∈ F2                       such that

                                               𝑦 + 𝐿(𝐴*pivot )𝑢 = 0.

For this choice of 𝑢, the representative 𝑤 + 𝐿(𝐴* )𝑢 is supported only on the free coordinates. Hence,
every equivalence class in 𝑊𝐴 has a free-supported representative.
   It remains to show this representative is unique. Suppose two free-supported vectors belong to
the same equivalence class in 𝑊𝐴 . Their difference is again free-supported and lies in im 𝐿(𝐴* ), so is
                                 |𝐺|𝑟
equal to 𝐿(𝐴* )𝑢 for some 𝑢 ∈ F2 1 . Looking at the pivot coordinates gives

                                                 𝐿(𝐴*pivot )𝑢 = 0

which implies 𝑢 = 0 since 𝐿(𝐴*pivot ) is invertible. This proves uniqueness.
                                𝛼,𝑔    𝛼,𝑔    𝛽,𝑔         𝛽,𝑔
   By Lemma 2, the vectors 𝑣𝐴       , 𝑤𝐴   , 𝑣𝐵   , and 𝑤𝐵    defined before Theorem 4 are well defined.
                                       𝛼,𝑔            𝛽,𝑔
Moreover, the binary expansions b(𝑣𝐴 ) and b(𝑣𝐵 ) form bases of 𝑉𝐴 and 𝑉𝐵 , and the equivalence
              𝛼,𝑔          𝛽,𝑔
classes of b(𝑤𝐴   ) and b(𝑤𝐵   ) form bases of 𝑊𝐴 and 𝑊𝐵 , respectively. The logical operators of
Theorem 4 are tensor products of these vectors, lifted to their binary representation. Since we are
taking tensor products over ℛ, we recall that they satisfy the balancing relation

                          𝑢𝑔 ⊗ℛ 𝑣 = 𝑢 ⊗ℛ 𝑔𝑣              for all 𝑢 ∈ ℛ𝑐1 , 𝑣 ∈ ℛ𝑐2 , 𝑔 ∈ 𝐺
                                                         𝛼,𝑔       𝛽,ℎ
by construction. Hence, a tensor product of the form 𝑣𝐴       ⊗ℛ 𝑤𝐵    is uniquely specified by the group

𝐴-side and the identity element 𝑒𝐺 is placed on the 𝐵-side. We also adopt the convention that 𝐴 ⊗ 𝐼𝑐2
acts on the first tensor factor by the left action, (𝐴 ⊗ 𝐼𝑐2 )(𝑢 ⊗ 𝑣) = (𝐴𝑢) ⊗ 𝑣, while 𝐼𝑐1 ⊗ 𝐵 acts on
the second factor by the right action, (𝐼𝑐1 ⊗ 𝐵)(𝑢 ⊗ 𝑣) = 𝑢 ⊗ (𝑣𝐵).
   We can now prove Theorem 4.

Proof of Theorem 4. Our strategy will be to show that the 𝑋-type and 𝑍-type representatives defined
above satisfy the hypotheses of Lemma 1. By Definition 10, the physical qubits of LP(𝐴, 𝐵) are

form a canonical basis for the 𝑘 = 𝑓𝐴 𝑓𝐵 |𝐺| single-qubit 𝑋 and 𝑍 logical operators of LP(𝐴, 𝐵).


                             3.    Aside on connection to Künneth formula

    One might notice that our canonical logical basis and its product structure is suspiciously reminis-
cent of the Künneth formula. This is not a coincidence, but rather a direct consequence of the square
invertibility condition. We now explain this connection.

                        C.    Universal fault-tolerant quantum instruction sets

   In this appendix, we provide a formal definition of the notion of a universal fault-tolerant quantum
instruction set. We then more formally define the three instruction sets we considered in the main
text and explain how they enable universal quantum computation.
   Consider a qLDPC processor based on a CSS code with parameters J𝑛, 𝑘, 𝑑K together with a fixed
decoder. An instruction is a logical operation on the processor’s logical qubits together with a physical
gadget that realizes the operation.

Definition 17 (Universal fault-tolerant quantum instruction set (formal version of Definition 1)).
Consider a qLDPC processor with 𝑘 logical qubits. A set of fault-tolerant instructions ℐ is a universal
fault-tolerant quantum instruction set for the processor if every unitary 𝑈 on the 𝑘 logical qubits can
be realized to any precision 𝜖 > 0 by a finite sequence of instructions from ℐ [109], where

   1. each instruction may be chosen adaptively, conditioned on the classical record of earlier mea-
      surement outcomes, and

   2. the combined action of the sequence is required to equal 𝑈 only up to a Pauli frame determined
      by that record.

   An example of a universal instruction set is the collection of two-qubit CNOTs together with the
single-qubit Hadamard and 𝑇 gates [63]. Universality, however, does not require unitary gadgets;
measurements and state injection suffice, as the constructions below show.

Definition 18 (Basic instruction set ℐ𝐵 ). The basic instruction set consists of (i) the single-qubit
logical Pauli measurements {𝑋         ¯ 𝑖 , 𝑌¯𝑖 , 𝑍¯𝑖 }𝑘 , (ii) the weight-two logical Pauli-product measurements
                                                       𝑖=1
{𝑋¯𝑖𝑋¯ 𝑗 , 𝑍¯𝑖 𝑍¯𝑗 }1≤𝑖<𝑗≤𝑘 , and (iii) noisy 𝑇 -state injection i.e. the preparation of a designated logical
qubit in the state 𝑇 |+⟩ up to a known Pauli correction, with an injection error rate set by the physical
noise.

The Clifford group together with the 𝑇 gate forms a universal gate set [63, 64]. Moreover, any
Clifford+𝑇 circuit can be compiled into a Pauli-based computation consisting of stabilizer-state prepa-
rations, adaptive logical Pauli-product measurements, and the consumption of logical |𝑇 ⟩ states [65].
Noisy logical |𝑇 ⟩ states are injected using instruction (iii) and are then distilled. Provided the input
error lies below the distillation threshold and the logical stabilizer operations are sufficiently reliable,
magic-state distillation can reduce the output error below any target 𝜖 > 0 with overhead polylog-
arithmic in 1/𝜖 [64, 67, 110]. The distillation circuit may be implemented either using transversal
CNOT gates between separate encoded blocks or, when a single block encodes many logical qubits,
as a sequence of logical Pauli-product measurements within that block [68]. A distilled |𝑇 ⟩ state is
then consumed via gate teleportation to implement the logical 𝑇 gate [22, 64]. These ingredients are
standard; what is specific to the mitten codes is that every instruction in ℐ𝐵 is implemented using
only five reusable gadgets (Section IV, Table II).

Definition 19 (High-throughput instruction set ℐ𝐻 ). The high-throughput instruction set consists
of (i) parallel logical Pauli-product measurement i.e. the joint fault-tolerant measurement of a set
of commuting logical Pauli products in a single gadget, and (ii) parallel 𝑇 -state injection i.e. the
simultaneous injection of noisy 𝑇 states into all 𝑘 logical qubits of a block.

Definition 20 (Fixed-gadget instruction set ℐ𝐹 ). The fixed-gadget instruction set consists of (i) the
extractor measurement, i.e., the fault-tolerant measurement of an arbitrary logical Pauli product, and
(ii) noisy 𝑇 -state injection. The extractor is a single fixed gadget: its qubit layout and connectivity
are fixed in hardware, and the Pauli product to be measured is selected in software by switching the
appropriate connections on or off.

Every instruction of the basic instruction set ℐ𝐵 can be viewed as a special case of an instruction of
the other two sets. Therefore, both ℐ𝐻 and ℐ𝐹 are universal fault-tolerant quantum instruction sets.

                                                             D.    Surgery

   Universal quantum computation can be achieved with Clifford gates and non-Clifford magic state
inputs [64, 65]. On qLDPC codes, Clifford computation can be implemented through logical Pauli-
product measurements (PPMs) generated via code surgery [52, 73, 75–77]. Code surgery realizes
PPMs on general qLDPC codes by introducing a surgery system: ancillary qubits and checks that
couple to the targeted logical operator are attached, temporarily enlarging the original code to realize
the desired measurement.
   Surgery can be viewed at the level of the merged code: the CSS code whose physical qubits are
the original data qubits together with the ancilla qubits of the surgery gadget. The checks of the
merged code are the original checks, some of which are deformed to attain support on the ancilla
qubits, together with new checks introduced by the gadget. A logical measurement then proceeds in
three steps: First, the ancilla qubits are initialized in |0⟩ for an 𝑋-type measurement (in |+⟩ for a
𝑍-type measurement), so that every deformed check initially carries the value of the original check it
extends, and every new check supported on ancilla qubits alone is initially deterministic. Second, the
checks of the merged code are measured for 𝑅 = 𝒪(𝑑) syndrome-extraction rounds (Appendix C). By
construction, a product of a fixed subset of the new checks yields the targeted logical operator, so the
                                                                                                                 41


product of the measurement outcomes for these checks returns the logical measurement result, and
repeating for 𝑅 rounds protects this result against measurement errors. Third, the ancilla qubits are
measured in their initialization basis, restoring the original code; the outcomes fix the values of the
restored checks and determine a Pauli-frame update on the data block. Fault tolerance of the whole
procedure thereby reduces to two properties of the merged code: it must remain LDPC, so the check
weight of the merged code (which is lower bounded by the check weight of the original code plus one)
should not grow much compared to the original code. And its distance should not drop too much
below that of the original code – ideally the distance of the original code is preserved in the merged
code. These two requirements guide all constructions in this section.
   This section is organized as follows (Figure 5). In Appendix D.1 we construct the graph surgery
gadgets S𝑋 , S𝑌 , S𝑍 , S𝑋𝑋 , and S𝑍𝑍 for mitten codes (Figure 5(a) and (b)). An unstructured canonical
logical basis would require one dedicated gadget per measured operator, which means 2𝑘 seed gadgets
for the basis single qubit 𝑋  ¯ and 𝑍¯ operators and, from them, 3𝑘 + 𝑘(𝑘 − 1) distinct gadgets to
support ℐ𝐵 . The group structure of the canonical basis of mitten codes (Appendix B) reduces this to
5 reusable gadgets generated from only 2 seed gadgets, S𝑋 and S𝑍 , and rewiring a seed gadget by the
group action 𝑔 as illustrated in Figure 5(a) measures every operator in the 𝐺-orbit of its target, and
bridging seed gadgets produces the remaining three gadgets. In Appendix D.2 we construct parallel
surgery gadgets (Figure 5(c)), which measure many logical Pauli products on a single merged code to
support ℐ𝐻 . Because the canonical basis arises from the two classical factor codes, all canonical 𝑋
(𝑍) representatives, and hence the 𝑋-side (𝑍-side) parallel gadgets, are supported on only 2 of the 5
blocks of data qubits, 𝐷1 ∪𝐷2 (𝐷1 ∪𝐷3 ) of mitten codes, reducing the overhead of the parallel surgery
gadget. In Appendix D.3 we construct and bridge an 𝑋-side and a 𝑍-side extractor into a full extractor
(Figure 5(d)), which is a single fixed gadget measuring arbitrary logical Pauli products [59, 71] to
support ℐ𝐹 . Appendix D.4 collects the expansion conditions under which all these gadgets preserve the
code distance, and the methods we use to certify them. Throughout, every construction is presented
concretely for the mitten codes, whose canonical basis {𝑋    ¯ 𝑔 , 𝑍¯𝑔 }𝑔∈𝐺 consists of a single orbit pair
(Theorem 1). We also explain at the end of each subsection how the constructions generalize to LP
codes of other base-matrix shapes.


                                                1.   Graph surgery

   Without exploiting any structure of the code, supporting ℐ𝐵 typically requires one dedicated gadget
per measured operator: 3𝑘 distinct gadgets for the single-qubit measurements {𝑋        ¯ 𝑖 , 𝑌¯𝑖 , 𝑍¯𝑖 }𝑖∈[𝑘] , plus
𝑘(𝑘 − 1) distinct gadgets for the weight-two products {𝑋    ¯ 𝑗 , 𝑍¯𝑖 𝑍¯𝑗 }𝑖<𝑗 . Moreover, the size of each
                                                          ¯𝑖𝑋
gadget grows with the weight of the measured logical representative [52, 77]. For a generic qLDPC
code one only controls the weight of some spanning set of logical operators, and having a canonical
basis usually makes the representatives have much larger weights. Both the number and the size of
the gadgets therefore become less favorable.
   In contrast, the low-weight canonical logical basis we have explicitly constructed for the mitten
codes as in Appendix B:
                                            𝑔      𝑔

allows us to construct surgery gadgets in an economical way. To be more specific, for lifted product
codes satisfying the square invertibility condition in Definition 16, we can construct a canonical logical

basis with group structure, i.e. the operators within each set of {𝑋𝛼,𝛽    }, {𝑌𝛼,𝛽 } and {𝑍𝛼,𝛽 } with the
same 𝛼, 𝛽 are related by group actions, so the single basis Tanner graph masked to each specific logical
representative is the same.

   In the following, S(𝑃 ), abbreviated as S𝑃 , denotes the surgery gadget that measures the logical
Pauli operator 𝑃 . For any representative 𝑃 , let supp(𝑃 ) denote its support, and let X(𝑃 ) and Z(𝑃 )
denote the sets of 𝑋- and 𝑍-checks, respectively, whose supports intersect supp(𝑃 ). For an 𝑋-type
representative 𝑃 , the graph surgery gadget is determined by two pieces of data: (i) the sets supp(𝑃 )
and Z(𝑃 ), and (ii) the bipartite adjacency between them inherited from 𝐻𝑍 . We describe only this
𝑋-type case, as the construction for a 𝑍-type representative is obtained symmetrically by exchanging
𝑋 and 𝑍. For the mitten codes, the relevant opposite-type checks are localized by Eq. (2): Z(𝑋                    ¯ 𝑔 ) is
                                                   ¯
contained in the check block 𝑍0 , while X(𝑍𝑔 ) is contained in 𝑋0 .
   The gadget S(𝑋     ¯ 𝑔 ) is built in two stages. The first stage is its skeleton, which is the interface
between the gadget and the code that specifies which logical operator is measured. The skeleton
contains one new 𝑋-check for every qubit in supp(𝑋           ¯ 𝑔 ) that is attached transversally (each new check
acts on its own data qubit), and one ancilla qubit for every 𝑍-check in Z(𝑋              ¯ 𝑔 ) that is also attached
transversally (each such 𝑍-check is deformed onto its own ancilla qubit). Within the skeleton, a new
𝑋-check acts on an ancilla qubit exactly when the corresponding data qubit lies in the support of
the corresponding 𝑍-check. This connectivity helps to make the merged code a valid CSS code as a
new 𝑋-check and a deformed 𝑍-check overlap on a data qubit precisely when they also overlap on the
paired ancilla qubit, hence always on an even number of qubits. Moreover, since 𝑋                ¯ 𝑔 ∈ ker 𝐻𝑍 , every
𝑍-check in Z(𝑋  ¯ 𝑔 ) overlaps supp(𝑋   ¯ 𝑔 ) evenly, so every ancilla qubit is acted on by an even number of
new 𝑋-checks and the product of all new 𝑋-checks is therefore supported on the data qubits alone,
where it equals 𝑋   ¯ 𝑔 . Measuring the new checks thus reads out the target logical operator through
the protocol described at the beginning of this section. It is natural to view the new 𝑋-checks as
vertices and each ancilla qubit as a hyperedge on the set of vertices fixed by its 𝑍-check. Since the
skeleton is in general a hypergraph, we then replace each hyperedge by a set of ordinary edges and
deform the corresponding 𝑍-checks. This decomposition preserves all the properties above and makes
the interface an ordinary graph. The second stage makes the gadget fault tolerant, where we add
further edges (ancilla qubits) on top of the skeleton to raise the expansion of the gadget graph up to
the distance-preserving condition of Appendix D.4, and at the same time introduce one new 𝑍-check
for every independent cycle of the final graph so that the gadget contributes no logical qubits of its
own. We also optimize the construction to keep vertex degrees and cycle lengths bounded so that the
merged code remains LDPC. The merged code then encodes 𝑘 − 1 logical qubits, with 𝑋                     ¯ 𝑔 promoted
to a stabilizer. For S𝑋 , the columns of Table II count exactly these objects: 𝑛anc edges and 𝑚𝑋
vertices of a connected gadget graph together with its 𝑚𝑍 = 𝑛anc − 𝑚𝑋 + 1 independent-cycle checks
(symmetrically for S𝑍 with the two check types exchanged).
   To be more explicit, we order the qubits of the merged code as the 𝑛 data qubits followed by
the ancilla qubits, and encode a gadget with vertex checks 𝒱, ancilla qubits (edges) ℰ, and a basis

mod-2 boundary matches its overlap with the attached data qubits which is precisely the rule of the
skeleton construction and its hyperedge decomposition. The skeleton itself is the special case where
Γ has one hyperedge per check in Z(𝑋           ¯ 𝑔 ) and 𝐽 deforms each of these checks onto its own ancilla
qubit. In this block form, the product of the vertex checks over a subset 𝑇 ⊆ 𝒱 acts as 𝑋 on the
data qubits indicated by Π𝑇 1𝑇 and on the ancilla qubits indicated by Γ1𝑇 , so the operators read
out by the gadget are the 𝑋 operators supported on Π𝑇 𝑥 for 𝑥 ∈ ker Γ. For a connected gadget
graph, ker Γ = {0, 1𝒱 } and the unique measured operator is 𝑋            ¯ 𝑔 , while for the hypergraph gadgets of
Appendix D.2 the kernel is larger by design. The same block form describes the bridged gadgets S𝑋𝑋
and S𝑍𝑍 , whose gadget graph is the disjoint union of two seed graphs joined by the bridge edges, as
well as 𝑍-type measurements after exchanging the roles of 𝐻𝑋 and 𝐻𝑍 . The only exception is S𝑌 ,
whose mixed-type bridge checks act as 𝑋 towards the S𝑋 side and as 𝑍 towards the S𝑍 side, so that
its merged code is a stabilizer code but not a CSS code. Finally, note that Γ is exactly the incidence
matrix whose expansion enters the distance-preserving condition of Appendix D.4 where the single
object Γ determines both what the gadget measures, through ker Γ, and whether the merged code
preserves the code distance, through its Cheeger constant.
    The group symmetry of the mitten-code Tanner graph makes these gadgets reusable. Recall that
the blocks of 𝐻𝑋 and 𝐻𝑍 are regular representations of ring elements (Equation (2)), and that an
entry of a right-representation block, 𝑅(𝑏)𝑠,𝑞 , depends on the group labels 𝑠 of the check and 𝑞 of the
qubit only through 𝑠−1 𝑞. It is therefore invariant under the simultaneous left group action 𝑞 ↦→ 𝑔𝑞,
𝑠 ↦→ 𝑔𝑠 of all qubit and check labels (and likewise left-representation blocks are invariant under right
translations). The representatives 𝑋     ¯ 𝑔 are supported on 𝐷1 ∪ 𝐷2 , on which the 𝑍-checks of 𝑍0 act only
through the right-representation blocks 𝑅(𝑏0 ) and 𝑅(𝑏1 ), and they satisfy supp(𝑋             ¯ 𝑔 ) = 𝑔·supp(𝑋 ¯ 𝑒 ) and
Z(𝑋 ¯ 𝑔 ) = 𝑔 · Z(𝑋¯ 𝑒 ) under left group action. Consequently, as illustrated in Figure 5(a), the bipartite
adjacency between supp(𝑋      ¯ 𝑔 ) and Z(𝑋¯ 𝑔 ) is the same for all 𝑔 ∈ 𝐺, so the skeleton, and with it the entire
gadget, can be kept fixed: the single seed gadget S𝑋 = S(𝑋             ¯ 𝑒 ) measures every 𝑋     ¯ 𝑔 after rewiring its
interface, i.e. attaching the vertex check paired with data qubit 𝑞 to 𝑔𝑞 instead, and the ancilla edges
deforming check 𝑠 to 𝑔𝑠 instead. Right group actions give the analogous statement for the seed gadget
S𝑍 = S(𝑍¯𝑒 ), since the 𝑋-checks of 𝑋0 act on 𝐷1 ∪ 𝐷3 only through the left-representation blocks
𝐿(𝑎0 ) and 𝐿(𝑎1 ). The remaining gadgets are obtained by bridging seed gadgets [52, 75]. To measure
a product 𝑋      ¯ ℎ , we attach two rewirings of S𝑋 and join their graphs by a bridge of additional ancilla
              ¯𝑔 𝑋
qubits (edges between the two vertex sets), together with one new 𝑍-check for each new independent
cycle. The bridged graph is connected, so the only product of vertex checks acting trivially on the
ancilla qubits is the product of all of them, and the merged code measures exactly the product 𝑋                   ¯𝑔 𝑋¯ℎ
rather than the individual factors. The bridge must contain at least 𝑑 edges to satisfy the distance-
preserving property and the product of the vertex checks of one seed gadget equals its factor times
𝑋 on the bridge, so each factor is equivalent, in the merged code, to an operator supported on the
bridge alone. To measure 𝑌¯𝑔 , which up to a phase is the product 𝑋             ¯ 𝑔 𝑍¯𝑔 , we bridge S𝑋 with S𝑍 ; the

                                              2.   Parallel surgery

   As illustrated in Figure 5(c), we also construct parallel surgery8 gadgets, following the framework
of [55], to support ℐ𝐻 . Surgery can be viewed as a homological measurement [76]: the operators
read out by a gadget are the products of its vertex checks that act trivially on the ancilla qubits, i.e.
products over vertex subsets having even overlap with every ancilla (hyper)edge. For a connected
gadget graph this space is one-dimensional since the only nontrivial such subset is the full vertex set,
which is why each graph surgery gadget of Appendix D.1 measures exactly one logical Pauli product.
Allowing the gadget to be a hypergraph removes this restriction: the space of vertex subsets with

8
    Also known as high-rate surgery.
                                                                                                         45


even overlap with every hyperedge can have any dimension 𝑛𝑝 ≥ 1, so a single merged code can
measure 𝑛𝑝 independent logical Pauli products at once, achieving a constant information-extraction
rate in the sense of [55, Definition 2]. On the mitten codes we use such gadgets to measure up to
𝑘/2 disjoint weight-two products of the form 𝑋       ¯ ℎ , covering all 𝑘 = |𝐺| logical qubits of a block in
                                                  ¯𝑔 𝑋
a single parallel measurement.
    Concretely, given 𝑛𝑝 independent logical Pauli products – where for the mitten codes we consider
products of the canonical representatives 𝑋  ¯ 𝑔 , whose supports all lie within 𝐷1 ∪ 𝐷2 so that every
deformed 𝑍-check lies in 𝑍0 – we form the union of their supports and build the skeleton exactly as
in graph surgery. In the skeleton, a new 𝑋-check is added for each qubit in the union and attached
to the data qubits transversally. An ancilla qubit is added for each 𝑍-check acting on the union,
deforming that check to include the ancilla. The connectivity among the added checks and ancilla
qubits is inherited from the Tanner graph of the code. The only difference from graph surgery is
that we do not decompose the resulting hyperedges in the skeleton into edges. Instead, the skeleton
remains a hypergraph. For each target product, the new 𝑋 checks over its support multiply to yield
that product – ancilla contributions cancel because each target lies in ker 𝐻𝑍 . Therefore, all 𝑛𝑝
operators are measured on the same merged code. Starting from this skeleton, we perform adaptive
thickening as in [55], which serves two purposes at once. First, it raises the expansion of the gadget
towards the distance-preserving condition of Appendix D.4. Second, it gauges out the spurious logical
degrees of freedom that a hypergraph ancilla system can otherwise introduce. The final gadgets,
whose parameters are reported in Table III, add exactly 𝑛𝑝 more new checks than ancilla qubits, so
each merged code encodes exactly 𝑘 − 𝑛𝑝 logical qubits and the 𝑛𝑝 measured products are promoted
to stabilizers, with no spurious logical operators remaining.
    The check matrices of these merged codes are again given by the block form of Equation (D1).
For the skeleton, Γ has one hyperedge per 𝑍-check acting on the union of the measured supports,
with Γ𝑠,𝑣 = 1 iff the data qubit attached to 𝑣 lies in the support of the 𝑍-check 𝑠, and 𝐽 deforms
each of these checks onto its own ancilla qubit, so the constraint Π𝐻𝑍𝑇 = Γ𝑇 𝐽 𝑇 holds by construction.
The indicator of the vertex subset over the support of each target product lies in ker Γ, which is
exactly what makes the 𝑛𝑝 products simultaneously measurable. Adaptive thickening then replaces
the gadget-internal blocks Γ𝑇 and Λ by the internal 𝑋- and 𝑍-check matrices of the thickened ancilla
system while keeping the interface blocks Π and 𝐽 on its first layer, and proceeds until no spurious
kernel element survives and the merged codes have distance at most two less compared to the original
code distance.
    As a concrete example, we construct parallel surgery gadgets on the J300, 60, 14K code that jointly
measure 𝑛𝑝 ∈ {10, 20, 30} randomly chosen, independent weight-two products of canonical Pauli-𝑋
logical operators. Table III presents the gadget overheads together with the certified distances of the
merged codes.

Remark 5 (Generalization of parallel surgery gadget to LP codes of general shape). For any LP code
satisfying the square invertibility condition, the same construction measures an arbitrary collection
                                                                    𝑔                          𝑔
of independent same-type products of the canonical operators 𝑋𝛼,𝛽       (or, symmetrically, 𝑍𝛼,𝛽 ). The
canonical 𝑋 basis occupies only the row blocks 𝛼 ≤ 𝑓𝐴 of the first block of qubits, i.e. at most 𝑓𝐴 𝑐2 of
the 𝑐1 𝑐2 + 𝑟1 𝑟2 ring blocks, and the 𝑍-checks acting on them lie in the corresponding 𝑓𝐴 𝑟2 ring blocks
of checks; the union of the measured supports, and hence the parallel gadget, attaches only there. The
skeleton, the adaptive thickening, and the sQetch-based certification carry over unchanged.


                                              3.   Extractor

   An extractor [59] as illustrated in Figure 5(d) is a fixed ancilla system, wired to the code block once,
in which individual connections can be activated or deactivated in software. Activating the connections
that address a chosen logical Pauli product turns the extractor into a surgery gadget measuring exactly
that product. A single fixed gadget thereby measures arbitrary logical Pauli products, realizing
instruction (i) of ℐ𝐹 without per-operator hardware reconfiguration. Generally, extractors are costly

in space, but it was shown for hypergraph product codes that the structure of the logical basis can
suppress the extractor overhead [71]. We follow the same principle where our extractors are organized
around the canonical logical basis of Appendix B, which is derived from the codewords of the two
classical factor codes 𝐴 and 𝐵 of LP(𝐴, 𝐵) and is therefore supported on few, fixed blocks of data
qubits.
    We first construct a single-side extractor for each Pauli type (𝑋 and 𝑍) and then bridge the two
into a full extractor. For a mitten code, every canonical 𝑋-basis representative is supported on the
two data blocks 𝐷1 ∪ 𝐷2 , and the only 𝑍-checks acting on these blocks are the |𝐺| checks of the
block 𝑍0 (Equation (2)). The single-side extractors therefore attach to 2 of the 5 data blocks rather
than to the whole code, which is what keeps their overhead low. The skeleton of the 𝑋-extractor
is the union of the graph-surgery skeletons of all canonical 𝑋 operators: one vertex 𝑋-check for
each of the 2|𝐺| qubits of 𝐷1 ∪ 𝐷2 , attached transversally, and one ancilla qubit for each of the |𝐺|
checks  of 𝑍0 , deformed transversally, with the hyperedge connectivity between them inherited from
the 𝑅(𝑏0 ) 𝑅(𝑏1 ) sub-block of 𝐻𝑍 . Activating only the vertex checks on supp(𝑋     ¯ 𝑔 ), or on the support
     (︀           )︀

of any product of canonical 𝑋 representatives, together with the ancilla qubits of the 𝑍-checks acting
on it reproduces exactly the skeleton of the corresponding surgery gadget, so this single fixed system
addresses every 𝑋-type logical Pauli product. As in graph surgery, the hypergraph interface is then
reduced to a graph, with each high-degree (hyper)connection expanded into a loop of ordinary edges
whose mod-2 boundary reproduces the original connectivity. We then iteratively add edges and cycle
checks to establish the expansion required by Appendix D.4 while gauging out all logical degrees of
freedom internal to the gadget. The 𝑍-extractor is built symmetrically on 𝐷1 ∪ 𝐷3 and the check
block 𝑋0 . Finally, the two single-side extractors are joined by a bridge patterned on the transpose of
a repetition code whose distance matches that of the code, so that any operator crossing between the
two sides has weight at least 𝑑 to enable the construction of a distance-preserving extractor. Later,
further ancilla qubits and checks are added until the full extractor is itself expanding. The bridged
full extractor makes mixed-type products (including 𝑌¯𝑔 ) measurable while preserving the distance
and introducing no extra logical operators. Since one extractor must remain distance preserving for
every one of its exponentially many activation patterns, its certification relies on the Cheeger-constant
bounds of Appendix D.4 rather than on direct distance estimation. Table IV summarizes the resulting
overheads for the mitten codes.

Remark 6 (Generalization to extractors of LP codes of general shape). For any LP code satisfying
the square invertibility condition, the canonical 𝑋 basis occupies the row blocks 𝛼 ≤ 𝑓𝐴 of the first
block of qubits, so the 𝑋-side extractor skeleton consists of 𝑓𝐴 𝑐2 |𝐺| vertex checks attached transversally
to those qubits and 𝑓𝐴 𝑟2 |𝐺| ancilla qubits deforming the 𝑍-checks acting on them; symmetrically, the
𝑍-side extractor attaches to the 𝑐1 𝑓𝐵 |𝐺| qubits of the column blocks 𝛽 ≤ 𝑓𝐵 hosting the canonical 𝑍
basis, with 𝑟1 𝑓𝐵 |𝐺| ancilla qubits deforming the corresponding 𝑋-checks. The reduction to a graph,
the expansion augmentation, the bridging of the two sides, and the Cheeger-based certification carry
over unchanged.

             4.   Distance-preserving condition for the surgery and extractor gadgets

   Throughout the construction of the surgery and extractor gadgets, we quantify fault tolerance
by the distance of the merged code. Ideally the merged code preserves the distance of the original
code, 𝑑(Merge) ≥ 𝑑. Achieving this while simultaneously minimizing the gadget overhead is the
central difficulty as usually reducing the number of ancilla qubits conflicts with achieving the distance-
preserving property. This subsection collects the sufficient condition we design towards, and the
methods we use to certify it or to certify 𝑑(Merge) directly. Unlike the previous subsections, nothing
here refers to the structure of the mitten codes; the conditions depend only on the attached gadget
and apply to any CSS data code, hence to every base-matrix shape alike.
   A sufficient condition for distance preservation is that the gadget, viewed through its Tanner
graph, is a sufficiently good expander [52, 55, 76, 77]. The mechanism is that the gadgets introduce
no logical qubits of their own, so the distance can only drop if multiplying a logical operator of
the original code by new stabilizers which are products of vertex checks over a vertex subset 𝑇 of
the gadget lowers its weight. Such a product removes at most |𝑇 | qubits from the data support
while adding |𝜕2 𝑇 | ancilla qubits, where 𝜕2 𝑇 is the mod-2 boundary of 𝑇 in the gadget (hyper)graph
defined below. Boundaryless subsets only exchange the logical operator for an equivalent one of the
original code. Hence, if |𝜕2 𝑇 | ≥ |𝑇 | for every 𝑇 of minimal weight modulo boundaryless subsets,
no such multiplication can decrease the weight, and the merged code is distance preserving. The
Cheeger constant defined below is exactly the largest constant 𝜌 such that |𝜕2 𝑇 | ≥ 𝜌 dist(𝑇, ker) for
the boundary map of the gadget, so the sufficient condition reads ℎ2 ≥ 1. Since our gadgets are
hypergraphs in general, we state the definition directly for hypergraphs.

Definition 21 (Cheeger constant for hypergraphs). With the notation above and 𝐻 ̸= 0, the Cheeger
constant of 𝑋 is
                                           |𝐻𝑥|
            ℎ2 (𝑋) :=      min                       ,     dist(𝑥, ker 𝐻) :=    min wt(𝑥 + 𝑦) .
                        𝑥∈F𝑉
                           2 ∖ker 𝐻
                                      dist(𝑥, ker 𝐻)                           𝑦∈ker 𝐻


Remark 7 (Cheeger constant for Graphs). Let 𝐺 = (𝑉, 𝐸) be a connected graph on 𝑛 vertices, not
necessarily regular; then every row of 𝐻 has weight 2, so |𝑒 ∩ 𝑆| is odd iff 𝑒 has exactly one endpoint
in 𝑆 and 𝜕2 𝑆 = 𝜕𝑆 is the usual edge boundary. So the Cheeger constant is:
                                                           |𝜕𝑆|
                                       ℎ2 (𝐺) =     min         = ℎ(𝐺),
                                                  0<|𝑆|≤𝑛/2 |𝑆|

    Indeed, under Definition 21 the identity in Remark 7 holds because, for a connected graph, ker 𝐻 =
{0, 1𝑉 }: for the indicator 𝑥 of 𝑆 ⊆ 𝑉 we have dist(𝑥, ker 𝐻) = min(|𝑆|, 𝑛 − |𝑆|) and 𝜕2 𝑆 = 𝜕2 (𝑉 ∖ 𝑆),
so every ratio is attained by a set of size at most 𝑛/2, recovering the standard graph Cheeger constant
ℎ(𝐺).
                                                                                                         48


Theorem 5 (Cheeger inequality, lower bound). Let 𝐺 = (𝑉, 𝐸) be a connected graph on 𝑛 vertices
with Laplacian, and let 0 = 𝜆1 < 𝜆2 ≤ · · · ≤ 𝜆𝑛 be its eigenvalues. Then
                                               𝜆2
                                                  ≤ ℎ(𝐺).
                                               2
Proof. See [111, Thm. 2.2].

   We certify the distance-preserving property with different methods for the different gadget families.
For the graph surgery gadgets and the full extractors (Figure 5(a), (b) and (d)) we can certify
expansion directly: for the smaller seed gadgets (e.g. S𝑋 and S𝑍 of the six smallest mitten codes in
Table II) we compute the Cheeger constant exactly, and for the larger gadgets we use the spectral
lower bound of Theorem 5 to certify the expansion properties of those surgery gadgets. For the
parallel surgery gadgets (Figure 5(c)) we instead certify the distance of the merged code directly
with the fast distance estimator sQetch (Appendix H) at every step of the construction, which is
faster and therefore permits many more optimization rounds. For the extractors, direct estimation is
not an option: a single extractor supports exponentially many patterns of activated and deactivated
connections, each defining a different merged code, so estimating, let alone exactly computing, the
distance of every merged code is intractable. Certifying the expansion of the fixed gadget once instead
guarantees the distance-preserving property simultaneously for all activation patterns, which is why
the extractor constructions are driven by exact or spectral bounds on the Cheeger constant.


                                   E.   Parallel magic state injection

    Using the group structure of the canonical logical basis (Appendix B) and the product structure
of the lifted product codes, we can inject 𝑘 = |𝐺| logical |𝑇¯⟩ states in parallel into a block of the
J𝑛, 𝑘, 𝑑K processor code LP(𝐴, 𝐵), through a magic factory and an ancillary lifted product code that
we call the magic port. The resulting |𝑇¯⟩
                                            ⊗|𝐺|
                                                  blocks can then be fed into a distillation factory based
on transversal CNOTs [56, 112]. For each processor block, the magic factory consists of |𝐺| copies
of a distance-𝑑rep unrotated surface code, each prepared in a noisy |𝑇¯⟩ state, and the magic port is
the ancillary code LP(Rep(𝑑rep ), 𝐵), where Rep(𝑑rep ) denotes the distance-𝑑rep repetition code viewed
over F2 [𝐺] by replacing each 1 in its check matrix with the identity 𝑒 ∈ 𝐺.
    As shown in Figure 6, the injection consists of two steps. First, a parallel 𝑍¯ 𝑍¯ measurement, realized
by a transposed repetition-code bridge (Bridge I), switches between the surface-code stack and the
magic port, moving all |𝐺| |𝑇¯⟩ states into LP(Rep(𝑑rep ), 𝐵). Second, a parallel 𝑋       ¯𝑋¯ measurement
(Bridge II) switches between the magic port and the processor mitten code, moving the |𝐺| states
into the mitten processor code LP(𝐴, 𝐵). Both switches can act on all |𝐺| logical qubits at once
precisely because of the group structure of the canonical logical basis (Appendix B). Bridge I consists
of |𝐺| copies of the transpose of the repetition code Rep(𝑑rep )𝑇 with 𝑍-type checks, and it attaches
to both sides in the same “transversal” way. On the surface-code side, each surface code is itself a
product of two repetition codes. Pairing each of the |𝐺| surface codes with one copy of Rep(𝑑rep )𝑇 , the
bridge attaches to the repetition-code factor along the orientation hosting the 𝑍¯ logical of that surface
code, so the total connectivity is |𝐺| copies of the identity map (Figure 6). On the magic-port side, the
same connectivity is most naturally described at the ring level: viewing the |𝐺| copies of Rep(𝑑rep )𝑇
collectively as a single copy of Rep(𝑑rep )𝑇 over F2 [𝐺], the bridge attaches to the Rep(𝑑rep ) factor of
LP(Rep(𝑑rep ), 𝐵) by the identity map. This realizes the parallel 𝑍¯ 𝑍¯ measurement. The second step
follows the same idea where Bridge II uses the code 𝐵 * over F2 [𝐺] with 𝑋-checks, it is attached along
the 𝐵 factors of the magic port and of LP(𝐴, 𝐵). This realizes the parallel 𝑋        ¯𝑋¯ measurement. One
can also view the two code-switching steps as instances of parallel surgery (Appendix D.2) in which,
thanks to the matching product structures of the three codes, the surgery gadgets are simple and
structured rather than obtained by randomized optimization as in Appendix D.
    In the remainder of this section, we first prove in Theorem 6 that the magic port code
LP(Rep(𝑑rep ), 𝐵) has distance min(𝑑rep , 𝑑𝐵 ). We then prove that the two merged codes Merge𝐼

Figure 6: Parallel magic state injection scheme for mitten processor code LP(𝐴, 𝐵), drawn for 𝑑rep = 3. Every
node represents one block of |𝐺| data qubits (circles), 𝑋-checks (triangles), or 𝑍-checks (squares), and the dark
blue nodes track the injected logicals: the noisy |𝑇¯⟩ states are grown in the surface codes, moved through the
magic port LP(Rep(𝑑rep ), 𝐵), and finally land in the mitten code. The |𝐺| distance-𝑑rep surface codes (left)
together form the lifted product LP(Rep(𝑑rep ), Rep(𝑑rep )). Bridge I, given by |𝐺| copies of the transposed
repetition code Rep(𝑑rep )𝑇 with 𝑑rep 𝑍-checks and 𝑑rep − 1 ancilla qubits per copy, attaches via a simple seam
surgery gadget (pink lines, row by row) to the surface-code stack and to the Rep(𝑑rep ) component of the magic
port LP(Rep(𝑑rep ), 𝐵), realizing the parallel 𝑍¯ 𝑍¯ measurement of the first switching step. Bridge II, the code 𝐵 *
over F2 [𝐺] using the right-regular representation with two 𝑋-check blocks and one ancilla block, also attaches
via a simple seam surgery gadget (pink lines, column by column) to the 𝐵 side of the magic port and to the
𝐵 side of the mitten code LP(𝐴, 𝐵), realizing the parallel 𝑋   ¯𝑋¯ measurement of the second step. These seam
surgery gadgets are similar in spirit to how lattice surgery is performed between surface codes. The dashed
strips depict the classical base codes underlying the three lifted product codes. In each strip, the hatched node
marks the bit associated with the relevant logical operator, while the pink arcs show how the bridges merge
these bits at the level of the classical base codes.


and Merge𝐼𝐼 , obtained by attaching Bridge I and Bridge II respectively, preserve this distance, which
establishes that the parallel magic state injection scheme of Figure 6 is distance preserving end to
end (Theorem 7). Finally, we report the overhead of the scheme on the eight mitten codes for several
values of 𝑑rep in Table V.

last step holding by the definition 𝑑 = min(𝑑𝑋 , 𝑑𝑍 ).
    Therefore, what is left to show the distance-preserving property is to analyze the 𝑍 distance for
Merge𝐼 and 𝑋 distance for Merge𝐼𝐼 . We start by introducing the following lemmas:
Lemma 3 (Cleaning logical support). Consider bridging the two LP codes LP(Rep(𝑑rep ), 𝐵), with 𝐵
satisfying the square invertibility condition (Definition 16), and LP(𝐴, 𝐵) by the bridge code 𝐵 * with
𝑋-type checks, attached along the 𝐵-factor side of both codes as described above. Then every logical
𝑋 class of the merged code Merge𝐼𝐼 contains a representative 𝑋   ¯ ⋆ supported only on LP(𝐴, 𝐵).
Proof. The proof consists of three steps: (i) the magic port LP(Rep(𝑑rep ), 𝐵) has a canonical logical
𝑋 basis supported entirely on the data qubits to which the bridge attaches; (ii) a logical 𝑋 operator of
Merge𝐼𝐼 with no support on the port cannot have support on the bridge ancillas either; (iii) combining
(i) and (ii), the port part of any logical 𝑋 operator can be moved through the bridge onto LP(𝐴, 𝐵)
by multiplying with 𝑋 stabilizers of Merge𝐼𝐼 .
    For step (i): since 𝐵 satisfies the square invertibility condition and Rep(𝑑rep ) also naturally satisfies
it by definition, Theorem 4 provides a canonical logical 𝑋 basis of LP(Rep(𝑑rep ), 𝐵) supported only
on the first row of its data-qubit grid, exactly the qubits to which the 𝑋-checks of the bridge attach
(Figure 6). For step (ii): suppose a logical 𝑋 operator 𝑋     ¯ ′ of Merge𝐼𝐼 has no support on the port but
nontrivial support on the bridge ancilla block. The deformed 𝑍-checks of the port act transversally
on the bridge ancillas and otherwise only on port qubits, so commuting with them forces the ancilla
support of 𝑋 ¯ ′ to vanish. Thus, any logical 𝑋 operator without port support is supported on LP(𝐴, 𝐵)
alone. For step (iii): let 𝑋   ¯ be any logical 𝑋 operator of Merge𝐼𝐼 . The bridge 𝑋-checks act on the
ancilla block through 𝑅(𝑏1 ), which is invertible (since 𝐵 satisfies the square-invertibility condition and
                             *

                                      F.    A qLDPC processor discovery pipeline

   As illustrated in Fig. 3, we present a discovery pipeline for qLDPC processors supporting all three
quantum instruction sets (ℐ𝐵 , ℐ𝐻 , and ℐ𝐹 ) based on general lifted product codes. Its inputs are the
specific properties required for a fault-tolerant qLDPC processor, effectively the target values for the
three processor parameters of Definition 3, namely the target processing capacity, throughput, and
cycle time, together with a physical hardware budget. The outputs of the qLDPC processor pipeline
are concrete processor codes with low-weight addressable logical bases, and their gadgets realizing the
instruction sets (ℐ𝐵 , ℐ𝐻 , ℐ𝐹 ) with atom movements and qubit layout matched to the target atom-
array or superconducting devices. Because each requirement enters as an adjustable input rather than
                                                                                                        55


being fixed in advance, the same pipeline can be re-run to design processors for different platforms or
computational tasks.
    The pipeline starts from the code family that the processor is built upon. The shape of the two
base matrices 𝐴 and 𝐵 sets a floor on the encoding rate by itself, so fixing the shape guarantees a rate
floor before any code is built: as in Eq. (A26), for 𝐴 ∈ ℛ𝑟1 ×𝑐1 and 𝐵 ∈ ℛ𝑟2 ×𝑐2 , the rate of LP(𝐴, 𝐵)
satisfies
                                         𝑘   (𝑐1 − 𝑟1 )(𝑐2 − 𝑟2 )
                                           ≥                      ,                                   (F1)
                                         𝑛       𝑟1 𝑟2 + 𝑐1 𝑐2
and once the shape is fixed, the block size 𝑛 = (𝑟1 𝑟2 + 𝑐1 𝑐2 )|𝐺| depends only on the group order |𝐺|.
The 1 × 2 shape we use most often for mitten codes guarantees a rate of at least 20%.
    Simulating a full memory experiment for every candidate is too slow; therefore, the pipeline filters
codes by their check weight, code distance, and circuit-level distance, and keeps only the survivors.
The cheapest test is the check weight, which we read straight off the base matrices. The code distance
is one of the most important parameters we filter on. As proved in Theorem 8, under the square-
invertibility condition of Definition 16, the distance of a quantum code is upper bounded by the
distances of its classical base codes, so we can discard weak candidates before ever constructing the
quantum code (Appendix G). To be more specific, we first check the square-invertibility condition,
and only preserve those satisfying it; then we consider the classical distance bounds based on the
pattern of the classical codes 𝐴 and 𝐵. For example, when 𝐺 is abelian, we can use distance bounds
for classical QC-LDPC codes (Appendix G), such as Theorems 10 and 11, as filters. When 𝐺 is non-
abelian and 𝑟1 = 𝑟2 = 1, we may use other distance bounds, such as the commutator-subgroup bound
of Proposition 4, as filters. We certify the survivors with sQetch, a fast GPU-based distance estimator
developed for this purpose that runs roughly 105 × faster than previous quantum CSS code distance
estimators (Appendix H). Finally, we account for errors that spread through the syndrome-extraction
circuit itself. Only codes that clear all three tests reach a full memory-experiment simulation with
our telescoping decoder.
    A high-rate code is more useful as a processor if each of its logical qubits can be addressed indi-
vidually with a low-weight operator, which is a prerequisite for all three instruction sets (ℐ𝐵 , ℐ𝐻 , ℐ𝐹 )
to be efficient. This is in general difficult for high-rate processors as the supports of the logical rep-
resentatives overlap with each other. We enforce this in the third step of the pipeline by keeping
only codes whose base matrices satisfy the square-invertibility condition (Definition 16), which guar-
antees a group-structured canonical logical basis (Appendix B). Every processor the pipeline returns
therefore has individually addressable logical operators by construction. From this basis we assemble
the logical machinery, namely the reusable surgery gadgets of ℐ𝐵 , the high-rate surgery and parallel
magic-state injection of ℐ𝐻 , and the full extractor of ℐ𝐹 (Appendix D and Appendix E), turning the
memory into a universal fault-tolerant processor. We establish the fault tolerance of these gadgets
with theoretical proofs and benchmark them with the telescoping decoder.
    The final stage of the pipeline matches each surviving code to its target hardware. On atom
arrays, the pipeline optimizes the placement, labeling, and movement of the atoms based on the
group and product structure of the mitten codes. We optimize and benchmark the cost for both
atom-array and superconducting layouts; this stage fixes the cycle time of the resulting quantum
processors (Appendix J). Validation at any stage can feed back to the earlier ones, as shown by the
dashed arrows in Fig. 3, so a design that misses a distance, decoding, or hardware target—such as
a shortfall in processing capacity, throughput, or cycle time—is retuned by changing the group, the
base matrices, or the schedule.
    Running the pipeline end to end yields the family of processors in Table I, with further families
in Table VI below; the explicit construction data for the processor codes are given in Table XIII in
Appendix K.

                              G.      Distance bounds for lifted product codes

   In this appendix we prove upper bounds on the distance of a lifted product code LP(𝐴, 𝐵) in terms
of algebraic properties of its base matrices 𝐴 ∈ ℛ𝑟1 ×𝑐1 and 𝐵 ∈ ℛ𝑟2 ×𝑐2 and of the group 𝐺. The
starting point is Theorem 8: whenever the binary matrices 𝐿(𝐴) and 𝑅(𝐵) have full row rank — which
holds in particular under the square invertibility condition of Definition 16, and hence for every base
matrix used in this paper (Remark 11) — the distances of the two classical base codes upper-bound the
                                                                                                       57


quantum distance. Any construction of a low-weight classical codeword therefore caps the quantum
distance, and the rest of the appendix gives three such constructions. The commutator subgroup
bound builds a codeword from the sum over the commutator subgroup [𝐺, 𝐺] and is strongest when
𝐺 is close to abelian. The element-order bound applies whenever some entry of a base matrix has the
form 1 + 𝑔, in which case the distance is at most the order of 𝑔. Finally, for abelian 𝐺 the base codes
are classical quasi-cyclic LDPC codes, and we adapt the permanent-based distance bounds known for
that family. These abelian bounds are tight: searching with our processor discovery pipeline and the
distance estimation algorithm sQetch (Appendix H), we find a J560, 112, ≤ 14K code that attains the
distance-14 ceiling for 2 × 4 base matrices of check weight at most 9.


                              1.   Classical and quantum code distance

    In this section we prove that the minimum distance of the two classical base codes 𝐴 and 𝐵
upper-bounds the distance of the lifted product code LP(𝐴, 𝐵) provided 𝐿(𝐴) and 𝑅(𝐵) have full
row rank. We also show that the full row rank condition cannot be removed in general by exhibiting
a counterexample where the classical base codes are not full row rank and have distance less than the
lifted product code. The precise statement is the following.

Theorem 8 (Classical distance upper bound of quantum distance). Let ℛ = F2 [𝐺] for a finite group
𝐺, and let 𝐴 ∈ ℛ𝑟1 ×𝑐1 and 𝐵 ∈ ℛ𝑟2 ×𝑐2 with 𝑟1 < 𝑐1 and 𝑟2 < 𝑐2 . Assume that the binary matrices
                                   𝑟 |𝐺|×𝑐1 |𝐺|                                𝑟 |𝐺|×𝑐2 |𝐺|
                          𝐿(𝐴) ∈ F21                    and      𝑅(𝐵) ∈ F22

have full row rank. Define the classical distances of the two base codes,

            𝑑𝐴 = min wt(𝑢) : 0 ̸= 𝑢 ∈ ker 𝐿(𝐴) ,              𝑑𝐵 = min wt(𝑣) : 0 ̸= 𝑣 ∈ ker 𝑅(𝐵) ,   (G1)
                     {︀                           }︀                      {︀                    }︀


where wt(·) is the Hamming weight of a binary vector. Let 𝐻𝑋 and 𝐻𝑍 be the check matrices of
LP(𝐴, 𝐵) (Definition 10), and define

                           𝑑𝑥 = min wt(𝑥) : 𝑥 ∈ ker 𝐻𝑍 ∖ rowspace(𝐻𝑋 ) ,
                                       {︀                                             }︀
                                                                                                     (G2)
                            𝑑𝑧 = min wt(𝑧) : 𝑧 ∈ ker 𝐻𝑋 ∖ rowspace(𝐻𝑍 ) ,
                                       {︀                                             }︀


where rowspace(𝐻) is the linear space spanned by the row of 𝐻, and 𝑑𝑄 = min(𝑑𝑥 , 𝑑𝑧 ). Then

                                            𝑑𝑥 ≤ 𝑑𝐵 ,         𝑑𝑧 ≤ 𝑑𝐴 ,                              (G3)

and hence

                                              𝑑𝑄 ≤ min(𝑑𝐴 , 𝑑𝐵 ).                                    (G4)

Remark 11. Note that the square invertibility condition of Definition 16 is a special case of the full
row rank condition, and so Theorem 8 applies to all codes we search for in this paper.

    We will prove 𝑑𝑥 ≤ 𝑑𝐵 by exhibiting an explicit logical 𝑋 operator 𝑋 ⋆ of weight 𝑑𝐵 ; the bound
𝑑𝑧 ≤ 𝑑𝐴 follows from an almost identical argument. The physical qubits of a lifted product code come
in two blocks, of sizes 𝑐1 𝑐2 |𝐺| and 𝑟1 𝑟2 |𝐺|, and so the support of an 𝑋 or 𝑍 type Pauli operator over
these physical qubits is naturally described as a pair of matrices over the group algebra ℛ = F2 [𝐺],
of shapes 𝑐1 × 𝑐2 and 𝑟1 × 𝑟2 respectively (Lemma 5 below makes this precise). In this language the
candidate operator 𝑋 ⋆ is easy to describe: take a minimum-weight codeword of the classical base code
𝐵 and place it in a single row of the 𝑐1 × 𝑐2 matrix, setting all other entries, and the entire 𝑟1 × 𝑟2
matrix to zero. The weight of 𝑋 ⋆ is then 𝑑𝐵 by construction, and it is straightforward to check that
it commutes with all the 𝑍 stabilizers.
    The real content of the proof is showing that the candidate 𝑋 ⋆ is not simply a product of 𝑋
stabilizers. The remainder of this section develops the proof in two steps. We first recast 𝑋 and 𝑍
                                                                                                                      58


type Pauli operators on the physical qubits as pairs of matrices over ℛ (Lemma 5), so that we may
work over the ring for the remainder of the proof. We then show that the full row rank condition
allows us to always construct such a candidate that is not a product of 𝑋 stabilizers (Lemma 6).
    Over the ring, the minimum-distance codewords of the two classical base codes come from the
kernels of the base matrices,

                         𝐾𝐴 := {𝑢 ∈ ℛ𝑐1 : 𝐴𝑢 = 0},                    𝐾𝐵 := {𝑣 ∈ ℛ1×𝑐2 : 𝑣𝐵 * = 0}.

In binary coordinates, Proposition 2(i) gives ker 𝐿(𝐴) = {b(𝑢) : 𝑢 ∈ 𝐾𝐴 } and ker 𝑅(𝐵) = {b(𝑣) : 𝑣 ∈
𝐾𝐵 }, so 𝑑𝐴 and 𝑑𝐵 are the minimum weights of b(𝑢) and b(𝑣) over nonzero 𝑢 ∈ 𝐾𝐴 and 𝑣 ∈ 𝐾𝐵 ,
respectively.
   To describe how we will construct logical operators from these minimum-weight codewords, it will
be helpful to express 𝑋 and 𝑍 type Pauli operators on LP(𝐴, 𝐵) as pairs of matrices over ℛ. The
physical qubits of LP(𝐴, 𝐵) come in two blocks, of sizes 𝑐1 𝑐2 |𝐺| and 𝑟1 𝑟2 |𝐺|, so an 𝑋 or 𝑍 type Pauli
operator is described by a binary vector split across these two blocks. Since the checks are built from
Kronecker products, they act most simply when each block of this vector is arranged as a matrix.
Accordingly, we (︀arrange the first block)︀ as 𝑉 ∈ ℛ𝑐1 ×𝑐2 and the second as 𝑈 ∈ ℛ𝑟1 ×𝑟2 , so that the full
binary vector is b(vec(𝑉 )), b(vec(𝑈 )) , where vec denotes the row-major flattening of (A27). In this
form, the checks act by left and right matrix multiplication.

    In light of Lemma 5, we identify the space of physical qubits with ℛ𝑐1 ×𝑐2 ⊕ ℛ𝑟1 ×𝑟2 and regard
ker 𝐻𝑋 , ker 𝐻𝑍 , rowspace(𝐻𝑋 ), rowspace(𝐻𝑍 ) as ring-level sets. Working in this picture will allow
us to construct our candidate logical operator and prove that it is indeed a logical operator and not
a product of stabilizers.
    Write 𝑒𝑖 for the 𝑖-th standard basis vector of ℛ𝑐1 that has the identity element 𝑒 in the 𝑖-th
position and zeros elsewhere. For 𝑔 ∈ 𝐺 and a row vector 𝑣𝐵 ∈ ℛ1×𝑐2 , the product 𝑒𝑖 𝑔 𝑣𝐵 ∈ ℛ𝑐1 ×𝑐2
is the matrix whose 𝑖-th row is 𝑔𝑣𝐵 and whose remaining rows are zero; matrices of this form will
correspond to the support of our candidate 𝑋 logical operators, which live entirely on the first block
of physical qubits. By Lemma 5(ii), the products of 𝑋 stabilizers are exactly the pairs (𝐴* 𝑆, 𝑆𝐵 * )
with 𝑆 ∈ ℛ𝑟1 ×𝑐2 , so if the pair (𝑒𝑖 𝑔 𝑣𝐵 , 0) were a product of 𝑋 stabilizers, then 𝑒𝑖 𝑔 𝑣𝐵 = 𝐴* 𝑆 for
some 𝑆. The next lemma shows that the row index 𝑖 and the group element 𝑔 can always be chosen
so that no such 𝑆 exists. Part (ii) of the lemma is the corresponding statement for the candidate 𝑍
logical operators. Writing 𝑓𝑗 for the analogous 𝑗-th standard basis row vector of ℛ1×𝑐2 , the candidate
(𝑢𝐴 𝑔𝑓𝑗 , 0) carries the column 𝑢𝐴 𝑔 in its 𝑗-th column, and the lemma chooses 𝑗 and 𝑔 so that it is
not among the 𝑍 stabilizer products (𝑇 𝐵, 𝐴𝑇 ), 𝑇 ∈ ℛ𝑐1 ×𝑟2 .

Lemma 6 (Avoiding the stabilizers). Suppose that 𝐿(𝐴) and 𝑅(𝐵) have full row rank, with 𝑟1 < 𝑐1
and 𝑟2 < 𝑐2 . Then:

  (i) for every nonzero row vector 𝑣𝐵 ∈ ℛ1×𝑐2 there exists 𝑖 ∈ {1, . . . , 𝑐1 } and 𝑔 ∈ 𝐺 such that
      𝑒𝑖 𝑔 𝑣𝐵 ̸= 𝐴* 𝑆 for any 𝑆 ∈ ℛ𝑟1 ×𝑐2 ;

 (ii) for every nonzero column vector 𝑢𝐴 ∈ ℛ𝑐1 there exists 𝑗 ∈ {1, . . . , 𝑐2 } and 𝑔 ∈ 𝐺 such that
      𝑢𝐴 𝑔𝑓𝑗 ̸= 𝑇 𝐵 for any 𝑇 ∈ ℛ𝑐1 ×𝑟2 .

Proof. We prove (i), the statement for 𝐿(𝐴); the proof of (ii) is similar but with the roles of left and
right multiplication exchanged.
   Since 𝐿(𝐴) is full row rank, Proposition 2(i) implies that the map 𝑢 ↦→ 𝐴𝑢 corresponding to left
multiplication by 𝐴 is a surjective map from ℛ𝑐1 onto ℛ𝑟1 . Let 𝑒𝑘 be the 𝑘-th standard basis vector
of ℛ𝑟1 that has the identity element 𝑒 in the 𝑘-th position and zeros elsewhere. By surjectivity we
may choose preimages 𝑠𝑘 ∈ ℛ𝑐1 with 𝐴𝑠𝑘 = 𝑒𝑘 for 𝑘 = 1, . . . , 𝑟1 ; collecting them as the columns of
𝑆𝐴 ∈ ℛ𝑐1 ×𝑟1 gives 𝐴𝑆𝐴 = 𝐼𝑟1 .
                                                         * 𝐴* = 𝐼 , so 𝑃 := 𝐼 − 𝐴* 𝑆 * satisfies
   Since the involution reverses products, this implies 𝑆𝐴        𝑟1      𝐴     𝑐1      𝐴

Since 𝑃𝐴 𝐴* = 0, the projector 𝑃𝐴 annihilates every matrix of the form 𝐴* 𝑆. Hence, it suffices to find
𝑖 and 𝑔 such that (𝑃𝐴 𝑒𝑖 𝑔) 𝑣𝐵 ̸= 0, since then 𝑒𝑖 𝑔 𝑣𝐵 cannot equal 𝐴* 𝑆 for any 𝑆.
   Suppose then, for contradiction, that (𝑃𝐴 𝑒𝑖 𝑔) 𝑣𝐵 = 0 for every 𝑖 and 𝑔. Form the orbit space

   Now view 𝑀𝐵⊕𝑐1 as the space of 𝑐1 × 𝑐2 matrices whose rows lie in 𝑀𝐵 . Any 𝑉 ∈ 𝑀𝐵⊕𝑐1 can be

   We can now assemble the proof of Theorem 8.

Proof of Theorem 8. We work in the ring picture of Lemma 5 and first prove 𝑑𝑥 ≤ 𝑑𝐵 . Pick 𝑣𝐵 ∈ 𝐾𝐵
such that wt(b(𝑣𝐵 )) = 𝑑𝐵 . By Lemma 6(i) there exists 𝑖 ∈ {1, . . . , 𝑐1 } and 𝑔 ∈ 𝐺 such that 𝑒𝑖 𝑔 𝑣𝐵 ̸=
𝐴* 𝑆 for any 𝑆 ∈ ℛ𝑟1 ×𝑐2 . Set

                                              𝑋 ⋆ := (𝑒𝑖 𝑔 𝑣𝐵 , 0).                                           (G8)

We claim that 𝑋 ⋆ is a nontrivial logical 𝑋 operator of weight 𝑑𝐵 . First, 𝑋 ⋆ commutes with all the 𝑍
stabilizers. Since 𝑣𝐵 ∈ 𝐾𝐵 ,

                                   (𝑒𝑖 𝑔 𝑣𝐵 )𝐵 * + 𝐴* · 0 = 𝑒𝑖 𝑔 (𝑣𝐵 𝐵 * ) = 0,

                                                                                      / rowspace(𝐻𝑋 ).
so 𝑋 ⋆ ∈ ker 𝐻𝑍 by Lemma 5(i). Second, 𝑋 ⋆ is not a product of 𝑋 stabilizers i.e. 𝑋 ⋆ ∈
If it were, then by Lemma 5(ii) its first component 𝑒𝑖 𝑔𝑣𝐵 would equal 𝐴 𝑆 for some 𝑆, contradicting
                                                                         *

the choice of 𝑖 and 𝑔. Hence 𝑋 ⋆ is a nontrivial logical 𝑋 operator. Finally, the matrix 𝑒𝑖 𝑔 𝑣𝐵 has a
single nonzero row, equal to 𝑔𝑣𝐵 , so in binary coordinates wt(𝑋 ⋆ ) = wt(b(𝑔𝑣𝐵 )) = wt(b(𝑣𝐵 )) = 𝑑𝐵 .
Hence 𝑑𝑥 ≤ 𝑑𝐵 .
    The bound 𝑑𝑧 ≤ 𝑑𝐴 follows from a similar argument, using Lemma 6(ii) to build

                                              𝑍 ⋆ := (𝑢𝐴 𝑔𝑓𝑗 , 0)                                             (G9)

from a minimum-weight 𝑢𝐴 ∈ 𝐾𝐴 . Combining the two bounds gives 𝑑𝑄 = min(𝑑𝑥 , 𝑑𝑧 ) ≤ min(𝑑𝐴 , 𝑑𝐵 ).


Remark 12. One might hope to prove an unconditional statement about the classical distance upper
bounding the quantum distance of the lifted product code. However, we present a counterexample that
demonstrates this is not possible and that one cannot in general get rid of the full row rank condition
in Theorem 8.
   Let 𝐺 = 𝐶4 × 𝐶2 = ⟨𝑥, 𝑦 | 𝑥4 = 𝑦 2 = 𝑒, 𝑥𝑦 = 𝑦𝑥⟩, so that |𝐺| = 8, let ℛ = F2 [𝐺], and consider the
base matrices 𝐴, 𝐵 ∈ ℛ3×4 given by

         1 + 𝑥 + 𝑥𝑦 + 𝑥3 𝑦     1 + 𝑦 + 𝑥𝑦 + 𝑥2 + 𝑥3         1 + 𝑥𝑦 + 𝑥2 𝑦             𝑥 + 𝑥𝑦 + 𝑥2 𝑦 + 𝑥3
     ⎛                                                                                                         ⎞

𝐴 = ⎝ 𝑦 + 𝑥2 + 𝑥2 𝑦 + 𝑥3           1 + 𝑥 + 𝑥2 𝑦             1 + 𝑥 2 𝑦 + 𝑥3        1 + 𝑦 + 𝑥 + 𝑥𝑦 + 𝑥3 + 𝑥3 𝑦 ⎠ ,
     ⎜                                                                                                         ⎟

       1 + 𝑦 + 𝑥2 𝑦 + 𝑥3 𝑦     1 + 𝑦 + 𝑥 + 𝑥3 + 𝑥3 𝑦     1 + 𝑥 + 𝑥2 𝑦 + 𝑥 3 𝑦           𝑥 + 𝑥3 + 𝑥3 𝑦

       𝑥𝑦 + 𝑥2 𝑦 + 𝑥3 + 𝑥3 𝑦     𝑥 + 𝑥𝑦 + 𝑥2 + 𝑥2 𝑦 + 𝑥3              𝑥𝑦 + 𝑥2 𝑦         𝑥𝑦 + 𝑥2 𝑦 + 𝑥3
     ⎛                                                                                                    ⎞

𝐵=⎝       𝑦 + 𝑥 + 𝑥2 + 𝑥3               1 + 𝑥 + 𝑥3             𝑥 + 𝑥 2 + 𝑥3 + 𝑥3 𝑦      𝑥 2 + 𝑥3 + 𝑥3 𝑦 ⎠ .
     ⎜                                                                                                    ⎟

            1 + 𝑥 + 𝑥3 𝑦                  𝑦 + 𝑥3                𝑦 + 𝑥 + 𝑥𝑦 + 𝑥3 𝑦      1 + 𝑥𝑦 + 𝑥2 + 𝑥3
                                                                                                           (G10)

Both matrices have the strictly wide shape required by Theorem 8, with 𝑟1 = 𝑟2 = 3 < 4 = 𝑐1 = 𝑐2
but do not satisfy the full row rank condition. Indeed, rankF2 𝐿(𝐴) = 22 and rankF2 𝑅(𝐵) = 23,
both strictly smaller than 𝑟1 |𝐺| = 𝑟2 |𝐺| = 24. Constructing bases for ker 𝐿(𝐴) and ker 𝑅(𝐵) and
enumerating all F2 -linear combinations of the basis vectors, we find that the classical distances of
the base codes are 𝑑𝐴 = 𝑑𝐵 = 8. Using integer programming, we then verified that the distance of
LP(𝐴, 𝐵) is exactly 10, so LP(𝐴, 𝐵) is a J200, 13, 10K code with

                                          𝑑𝑄 = 10 > 8 = 𝑑𝐴 = 𝑑𝐵 ,                                          (G11)

and the conclusion of Theorem 8 fails without the full row rank condition.

   We obtained the above counterexample by a guided randomized search over rank-deficient 3 × 4
matrices over F2 [𝐺] where 𝐺 = 𝐶4 ×𝐶2 . Let Ω = 𝑔∈𝐺 𝑔. Since 𝑔Ω = Ω for every 𝑔 ∈ 𝐺, multiplying Ω
                                               ∑︀

by any group-algebra element containing an even number of group elements gives zero; hence, if every
                                                                                                                         61


entry in one column of a base matrix has even weight, the vector supported on that column with entry
Ω is a codeword of binary weight |𝐺| = 8. In the matrices in the above counterexample this occurs in
the first column of 𝐴 and the third column of 𝐵, giving the codewords 𝑢𝐴 = 𝑒1 Ω = (Ω, 0, 0, 0)𝑇 ∈ 𝐾𝐴
and 𝑣𝐵 = Ω𝑓3 = (0, 0, Ω, 0) ∈ 𝐾𝐵 . We sampled the remaining entries randomly and rejected a
matrix unless exhaustive enumeration of its kernel showed that the planted codeword was its unique
minimum-weight codeword. After pairing candidate matrices 𝐴 and 𝐵, we rejected the pair whenever
any of the candidate operators (G8)–(G9) was not a stabilizer. Since 𝑔Ω = Ω, these candidate logical
operators reduce to 𝑋𝑖⋆ = (𝑒𝑖 Ω𝑓3 , 0) and 𝑍𝑗⋆ = (𝑒1 Ω𝑓𝑗 , 0), and we checked they were stabilizers by
solving 𝐴* 𝑆 = 𝑒𝑖 Ω𝑓3 and 𝑆𝐵 * = 0, and 𝑇 𝐵 = 𝑒1 Ω𝑓𝑗 and 𝐴𝑇 = 0, for every 𝑖 and 𝑗. We constructed
𝐻𝑋 and 𝐻𝑍 and used an exhaustive search to verify that every operator in ker 𝐻𝑋 and ker 𝐻𝑍 of
weight at most 𝐷 = max{𝑑𝐴 , 𝑑𝐵 } was a stabilizer, thereby certifying 𝑑𝑄 > 𝐷. Finally, we verified the
distance of LP(𝐴, 𝐵) with integer programming.


                                2.   The commutator subgroup bound

    We begin by recalling the definition of a commutator subgroup and establishing a few key proper-
ties.

Definition 22 (Commutator and commutator subgroup). Let 𝐺 be a finite group. For elements
𝑔, ℎ ∈ 𝐺, the commutator of 𝑔 and ℎ is

                                               [𝑔, ℎ] = 𝑔 ℎ 𝑔 −1 ℎ−1 .                                                (G12)

The commutator subgroup (or derived subgroup) of 𝐺 is the subgroup generated by all commutators:

                                         [𝐺, 𝐺] = ⟨ [𝑔, ℎ] : 𝑔, ℎ ∈ 𝐺 ⟩.                                              (G13)

The commutator subgroup [𝐺, 𝐺] is a normal subgroup of 𝐺, and the quotient 𝐺/[𝐺, 𝐺] is the abelian-
ization of 𝐺. The group 𝐺 is abelian if and only if [𝐺, 𝐺] = {𝑒}.

Definition 23 (Commutator subgroup sum). Let 𝐾 = [𝐺, 𝐺] denote the commutator subgroup of 𝐺.
Define the element
